CITE: Anytime-Valid Statistical Inference in LLM Self-Consistency
Hirofumi Ota, Naoto Iwase, Yuki Ichihara, Junpei Komiyama, Masaaki Imaizumi

Abstract
. Large language models often improve reasoning by sampling multiple outputs and aggregating their final answers, but precise and efficient control of error levels remains a challenging task. In particular, deciding when to stop sampling remains difficult when the stopping rule is data-dependent and the set of possible response labels is not known in advance. We study anytime-valid certification of a prespecified target answer as the unique mode of the model’s response distribution, a guarantee distinct from answer correctness. We propose the Certification by Intersection-union Testing with E-processes (CITE) algorithm, which provably controls false certification at any prescribed level under arbitrary data-driven stopping, without requiring prior knowledge of the answer category set. We also prove a category-set-size-free stopping-time rate, establish matching minimax lower bounds up to constants in the main regime, and extend the construction to confidence-weighted voting. Simulations and LLM self-consistency experiments show empirical error control and improved certification in diffuse-tail settings.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Background and Motivation

Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated increasingly strong performance on a wide range of tasks
Brown et al. 2020
;
Chowdhery et al. 2023
;
Achiam et al. 2023
;
Touvron et al. 2023
, especially from the use of
test-time compute
. Prompting methods such as chain-of-thought and least-to-most decomposition encourage models to expose intermediate reasoning steps, while inference-time strategies such as self-consistency and tree-of-thought style search improve robustness by sampling, exploring, and aggregating multiple candidate reasoning trajectories
(
Wei et al. 2022
;
Zhou et al. 2023
;
Wang et al. 2023
;
Yao et al. 2023
;
Brown et al. 2024
;
Snell et al. 2025
)
.

A notion of
adaptive self-consistency
has gained attention to reduce computational cost of sampling growing approximately linearly with the number of sampled trajectories.
A seminal approach is
majority voting

(
Wang et al. 2023
)
, which is simple, model-agnostic, and does not require a separate verifier, reward model, or additional training.
In particular, the method adapts to the case that
easy questions often stabilize after only a few samples, whereas hard questions remain ambiguous and may benefit from continued sampling. As extensions,
Aggarwal et al. 2023
studies a stopping method based on posterior agreement among sampled answers;
Li et al. 2024
uses low-entropy windows as a practical stopping signal;
Wang et al. 2025b
and
Wang et al. 2025a
allocate budgets using difficulty estimates or bandit-style uncertainty across sets of queries; and confidence-aware variants use response-level confidence to improve aggregation and stopping
Taubenfeld et al. 2025
;
Aghazadeh et al. 2025
.

One challenge of the literature is to handle the situation where we must precisely control error level
\varepsilon
of certifying a modal response, those that arise when the risk of failure is high.

A typical real example is the emergency-room triage on data such as ER-REASON
(
Mehandru et al. 2025
)
, where each LLM-output acuity score determines the time-to-physician for an arriving patient and each disposition determines admission-versus-discharge.
In this case, if a modal-response certificate is used operationally, the false-certification probability must be bounded at a prescribed level
\varepsilon
.
At present, it is difficult to control the certification errors with precision; therefore, keeping errors below a certain threshold requires performing an excessive number of inferences, which directly leads to a massive increase in computational costs.

Based on the situation, we have the following question:
can we derive a data-driven stopping rule for adaptive self-consistency that controls the error level of certifying a modal response by LLMs?

Figure 1.
Overview of the stopping rule by CITE. With the countably infinite set of categories
\mathcal{A}
, CITE tests the hypothesis of whether the target category
r\in\mathcal{A}
is a mode or not.

1.2.
Our Study

In this study, we propose the
Certification by Intersection-union Testing with E-processes
(CITE), which is an
anytime-valid
and
category-agnostic
sequential test for controlling the Type-I error of falsely certifying a prespecified response label as the unique mode.
We note the important features:
(i)
Anytime validity
: CITE works with data-driven stopping time and the error guarantee must hold uniformly over the time, not at a single prespecified sample size.
(ii)
Category-agnostic
, CITE works even when the response category is unknown a priori and grows as sampling proceeds, which can adapt to new responses from LLMs. CITE gives a stopping time based on the three conditions on empirical probabilities: (i) the target is larger than the runner-up, (ii) the target itself is large, and (iii) the unobserved category is small, using anytime-valid e-process machinery

(
Ville 1939
;
Grünwald et al. 2024
;
Ramdas and Wang 2025
)
. Figure
1
gives a graphical abstract.

Our contributions are as follows.

•

Anytime-valid category-agnostic error guarantee
:
CITE controls Type-I error of certifying a label as the unique mode uniformly over all
stopping times at any prescribed level
\varepsilon

(Theorem
4.1
).
The error guarantee is valid without the knowledge of answer categories.

•

Category-size-free stopping-time
:
We show that the stopping time by CITE has an order which is free from category size (Theorem
4.3
), which is

contrast to several baseline methods.
We also derive a matching
minimax lower bound
(Theorem
4.4
).

•

Extension to weighted case
:
We extend CITE to handle a case with weighted observations and gaps. We also derive an anytime-valid Type-I control and yield the same
rate of the stopping time (Section
5
).

•

Empirical performance
: Simulations and LLM experiments
(Section
6
) confirm that CITE controls
Type-I error empirically, matches or improves on baselines on three LLM benchmarks.

2.
Problem Formulation

2.1.
Setup and Observation

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a countable (possibly infinite) set of response
categories. We consider an
unknown categorical distribution
P
on
\mathcal{A}
, and write

p_{a}:=P(X=a)
for its probability mass function for
a\in\mathcal{A}
. Let

p_{(1)}\geq p_{(2)}\geq\cdots
denote the decreasing rearrangement
of
(p_{a})_{a\in\mathcal{A}}
, and let

\mathcal{F}_{t}:=\sigma(X_{1},\ldots,X_{t})
be the natural filtration.

Suppose that we observe an i.i.d. sequence
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots
from
P
:

Assumption 2.1

(i.i.d. categorical data)
.

(X_{t})_{t\geq 1}
are i.i.d. draws from a categorical distribution

P
on a countable set
\mathcal{A}
.

We define some notations for statistics from the observations. For each
a\in\mathcal{A}
and
t\geq 1
, let

N_{t}(a):=\sum_{i=1}^{t}\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=a\},\qquad\widehat{p}_{t}(a):=\frac{N_{t}(a)}{t},\qquad\mathcal{A}_{t}:=\{a\in\mathcal{A}:N_{t}(a)\geq 1\}

denote the count, empirical frequency, and set of observed
categories by time
t
.

Our definition of
P
has a distinctive feature: A may contain a countably infinite number of categories, and since
\mathcal{A}
is unknown, information about the set of categories cannot be used in decision-making. This setup is effective for representing the complex response patterns of LLMs.

2.2.
Statistical Testing

We study the fixed-target unique-mode testing problem.
For a target category
r\in\mathcal{A}
fixed
a priori
, i.e. independent
of the data, we design a sequential test of the
unique-mode hypothesis that
r
strictly dominates every other category.

Definition 2.2

(Null and alternative hypothesis)
.

For a fixed target category
r\in\mathcal{A}
, set

H_{0,r}:=\bigl\{P:p_{r}\leq\sup_{a\neq r}p_{a}\bigr\},\qquad H_{1,r}:=\bigl\{P:p_{r}>\sup_{a\neq r}p_{a}\bigr\}.

H_{1,r}
is the unique-mode alternative, i.e.
r
strictly dominates
every other category.
Under any
P\in H_{0,r}
there exists a deterministic witness
a^{\star}\neq r
such that
p_{a^{\star}}\geq p_{r}
holds.
A procedure
certifies

r
whenever it
rejects
H_{0,r}
in favor of
H_{1,r}
.

To handle this testing, we consider certifying the mode through a sequential procedure with the observations using the following criteria.

Definition 2.3

(Sequential certification procedure)
.

A sequential certification procedure for target
r
is a sequence

(D_{t})_{t\geq 1}
of
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-measurable decision rules, where

D_{t}=1
means that, after observing
X_{1},\ldots,X_{t}
, the procedure
certifies
r
as the unique mode. Its stopping time is

\tau:=\inf\{t\geq 1:D_{t}=1\},

with
\inf\emptyset=\infty
.
The procedure has anytime-valid Type-I error control at level
\varepsilon
if it holds that

\displaystyle\sup_{P\in H_{0,r}}\mathbb{P}_{P}(\tau<\infty)\leq\varepsilon.

(1)

If
\tau<\infty
, the procedure certifies
r
as the unique mode.

Importantly, the sampling time
t
is not fixed in advance, hence The bound (
1
) is time-uniform. At each time
t
, the
procedure either continues sampling or stops and certifies, using only the data observed so far.
The Type-I guarantee controls the probability that such a data-dependent rule ever falsely certifies
r
.

Remark 2.4
.

We note the originality of our setting. While similar settings have been considered in statistics
(
Good 1953
;
Painsky 2025
)
and LLM test-time compute
(
Aggarwal et al. 2023
;
Li et al. 2024
;
Komiyama et al. 2026
)
, to the best of our knowledge, an anytime valid sequential testing for the infinite and unknown category set has not been investigated. This setting describes with the nature of LLMs and represents a novel approach in terms of hypothesis testing.

3.
CITE: Certification by Intersection-union Testing with E-processes

We present our proposed method, CITE.
Here, the target
r\in\mathcal{A}
and the error level of certification
\varepsilon>0
is fixed before observing data as formalized in
Kim and Ramdas 2025
.
Our strategy involves using three data-driven stochastic processes to estimate the components of the distribution uniformly over time.
In particular, we consider
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-measurable stochastic processes
E_{t},L_{t}
and
U_{t}
with their forms will be derived in the following sections, which satisfies the following conditions under the null hypothesis
H_{0,r}
:

(1)

Pairwise evaluation
: For
a\in\mathcal{A}
,
E_{t}
evaluates an relative size of
p_{r}
against
p_{a}
, that is, for any
\alpha\in(0,1)
,
\sup_{t\geq 1}E_{t}\leq 1/\alpha
holds with probability at least
1-\alpha
.

(2)

Lower bound of the target probability
p_{r}
: the second process
L_{t}
bounds
p_{r}
below, that is,
\sup_{t\geq 1}L_{t}\leq p_{r}
holds.

(3)

Upper bound of unseen categories
: the third process
U_{t}
is an upper bound of probability of unseen categories, that is,
\inf_{t\geq 1}U_{t}\geq\sup_{a\in\mathcal{A}:N_{t}(a)=0}p_{a}
holds.

Note that these stochastic processes are calculated from observed values
X_{1},...,X_{t}
without the knowledge of
P
.

Using these processes, we define our stopping rule: if any of the conditions above satisfied by these stochastic processes is violated at time
t
, we reject the null hypothesis
H_{0,r}
and stop as
\tau=t
.

Rigorously, the stopping rule, with
P\in H_{0,r}
and
a^{\star}\neq r
satisfying

p_{a^{\star}}\geq p_{r}
, provides the following inclusion:

\{\tau<\infty\}\subseteq\underbrace{\bigl\{\exists t:E_{t}\geq 3/\varepsilon\bigr\}}_{\text{(i) pairwise}}\cup\underbrace{\bigl\{\exists t:L_{t}(r)>p_{r}\bigr\}}_{\text{(ii) target probability}}\cup\underbrace{\bigl\{\exists a,t:N_{t}(a)=0,p_{a}>U_{t}\bigr\}}_{\text{(iii) unseen categories}}.

(2)

In the following sections, we will define the stochastic processes
E_{t}
,
L_{t}
, and
U_{t}
in detail and evaluate these three events.

3.1.
Component (i): Pairwise E-Values

We give a specific form of the stochastic process
E_{t}
by the notion of e-process in the testing-by-betting style
of
Ramdas et al. 2023
.
For the fixed target
r\in\mathcal{A}
and a competing category
a\neq r
, we consider a centered indicator

Z_{i}^{(r,a)}:=\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=r\}-\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=a\}\in\{-1,0,+1\}
with a betting parameter
\lambda\in(0,1)
, and define the

pairwise e-process
as

{E}_{t}^{(r,a)}(\lambda):=\prod_{i=1}^{t}\bigl(1+\lambda Z_{i}^{(r,a)}\bigr)=(1+\lambda)^{N_{t}(r)}(1-\lambda)^{N_{t}(a)}.

(3)

The closed form depends on the data only through
(N_{t}(r),N_{t}(a))
.
Under the null hypothesis, i.e.
p_{r}\leq p_{a}
, it holds that
\sup_{t\geq 1}E_{t}^{(r,a)}(\lambda)\leq 1/\alpha
with probability at least
1-\alpha
; namely, it is a Nonnegative Super-Martingale (NSM) process.

We define the stochastic process
E_{t}
as the pairwise mixture e-process against the
empirical runner-up.
We extend
E_{t}^{(r,a)}(\lambda)
by integrating
\lambda
out.
Since the optimal
\lambda
is
(p_{r}-p_{a})/(p_{r}+p_{a})
(Proposition
C.6
) depends on unknown values,

we therefore mix over a finite grid
\Lambda_{\mathrm{pw}}\subset(0,1)
with positive weights
(w_{\lambda})
summing to at most

1
.
Namely, we define the pairwise mixture e-process as follows:

Definition 3.1

(pairwise mixture e-process)
.

For each competitor
a\neq r
, define

\displaystyle E_{t}={E}_{t}^{(r,\widehat{a}_{t})}\coloneqq\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{pw}}}w_{\lambda}E_{t}^{(r,\widehat{a}_{t})}(\lambda),\quad\widehat{a}_{t}:=\arg\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}_{t}\setminus\{r\}}N_{t}(a).

(4)

While
E_{t}^{(r,\widehat{a}_{t})}
is not a NSM process,
E_{t}^{(r,a)}
is NSM since it is a convex combination of NSM processes:

Corollary 3.2
.

Under
p_{r}\leq p_{a}
, for any
\alpha\in(0,1)
, we have
\sup_{t\geq 1}{E}_{t}^{(r,a)}\leq 1/\alpha
with probability at least
1-\alpha
.

3.2.
Component (ii): Lower Confidence Bound on the Target Probability
p_{r}

We develop a time-uniform lower confidence bound (LCB) on the target probability
p_{r}
to handle
hidden competitors
, i.e., categories
h\in\mathcal{A}
with
p_{h}>p_{r}
that have never appeared in the sample.

For each candidate value
q\in(0,1]
, we test a hypothesis “
p_{r}\leq q
” by
betting on the centered indicator
\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=r\}-q
, which is an analogous of

\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=r\}-\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=a\}
in Component (i). Fix a finite grid

\Lambda_{r}\subset(0,\infty)
with positive weights
(v_{\lambda})

summing to at most
1
, and for
q\in(0,1]
, we consider

\Lambda_{r}(q):=\{\lambda\in\Lambda_{r}:\lambda<1/q\},

thus each factor
1+\lambda(\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=r\}-q)
is positive.
Then, we define
the mixture e-process

{M}_{t}(q):=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{r}(q)}v_{\lambda}\prod_{i=1}^{t}\bigl(1+\lambda(\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=r\}-q)\bigr),

(5)

and it follows
\mathbb{P}(\sup_{t}M_{t}(q)\geq 1/\alpha)\leq\alpha
for any
\alpha\in(0,1)
under
p_{r}\leq q
(Corollary
3.2
).

Moreover,
q\mapsto M_{t}(q)
is
nonincreasing (Lemma
C.3
), so the set of

q
at which the wealth exceeds
1/\alpha
is a downset, making the following definition well-posed.

Definition 3.3

(Lower confidence bound on
p_{r}
)
.

Given
\varepsilon>0
, at time
t
, we define the lower confidence bound on
p_{r}
as

L_{t}=L_{t}(r):=\sup\Bigl(\{0\}\cup\bigl\{q\in(0,\widehat{p}_{t}(r)]:M_{t}(q)\geq 3/\varepsilon\bigr\}\Bigr).

Proposition 3.4

(LCB validity)
.

Under Assumption
2.1
, it holds that

\mathbb{P}\bigl(\exists t\geq 1:L_{t}(r)>p_{r}\bigr)\leq\varepsilon/3
.

3.3.
Component (iii): Unseen Upper Bound

We develop an upper bound for the probability of any currently unseen category, simultaneously over all categories and
times.
A category of mass
u\in(0,1)
remains unseen after
t
i.i.d. draws with probability

(1-u)^{t}
, and a
probability-weighted
union bound turns this
fact into a time-uniform
per-category
upper bound, bypassing
the need to estimate the aggregate missing mass.

Definition 3.5

(Unseen upper bound)
.

An upper bound of the unseen probabilities is defined as

U_{t}:=\min\bigl\{u\in(0,1]:u^{-1}(1-u)^{t}\leq\varepsilon/3\bigr\}.

The threshold balances two forces:
(1-u)^{t}
decays exponentially in

u
, while the
u^{-1}
factor
pays the union-bound cost over rare categories. As a result, we obtain the following guarantee:

Proposition 3.6

(Simultaneous validity of the unseen bound)
.

Under Assumption
2.1
, we obtain

\mathbb{P}\bigl(\exists a\in\mathcal{A},\ \exists t\geq 1:N_{t}(a)=0\ \text{and}\ p_{a}>U_{t}\bigr)\leq\varepsilon/3.

3.4.
Stopping Rule

We define the CITE stopping rule by combining these three stochastic processes above, which are uniform in time.
In particular, we design the stopping rule to follow the inclusion of the events as (
2
).
Algorithm
1
shows the pseudo-code of the CITE stopping rule.

Definition 3.7

(CITE stopping rule)
.

With given
\varepsilon>0
, CITE stops at the first time both

(a) Pairwise rejection:

E_{t}\geq 3/\varepsilon

at the empirical runner-up,
and
(b) LCB exceeds unseen bound:

L_{t}>U_{t}
,
hold simultaneously, i.e. we define the stopping time as

\tau:=\inf\bigl\{t\geq 1:E_{t}\geq 3/\varepsilon\text{ and }L_{t}>U_{t}\bigr\}.

(6)

If
\tau<\infty
, the procedure certifies
r
as the unique mode.

Algorithm 1

Stopping rule by CITE

Input:

target label
r\in\mathcal{A}
;
parameter grid
\Lambda_{\mathrm{pw}}\subset(0,1)
with weights
(w_{\lambda})
;
LCB grid
\Lambda_{r}\subset(0,\infty)
with weights
(v_{\lambda})
;
error level
\varepsilon>0
.

Output:

stopping time
\tau
and certification decision.

1.

Initialise

N_{0}(a)\leftarrow 0
for all
a\in\mathcal{A}
;

E_{0}^{(r,a)}(\lambda)\leftarrow 1
for all
a\neq r
,

\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{pw}}
.

2.

For

t=1,2,\ldots

do

2a.

Draw
X_{t}
and set

N_{t}(a)\leftarrow N_{t-1}(a)+\mathbf{1}\{X_{t}=a\}

for all
a\in\mathcal{A}
.

2b.

Step(i): Pairwise e-processes.

If
\mathcal{A}_{t}\setminus\{r\}=\emptyset
, set

P_{t}^{\mathrm{pw}}\leftarrow\texttt{true}
(the unseen
condition
U_{t}
then governs whether
\tau=t
) and skip
to Step (II);

otherwise, set

\widehat{a}_{t}\leftarrow\arg\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}_{t}\setminus\{r\}}N_{t}(a)

and, for each
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{pw}}
, compute

E_{t}=E_{t}^{(r,\widehat{a}_{t})}\leftarrow{\textstyle\sum_{\lambda}}w_{\lambda}E_{t}^{(r,\widehat{a}_{t})}(\lambda),\qquad E_{t}^{(r,\widehat{a}_{t})}(\lambda)\leftarrow(1+\lambda)^{N_{t}(r)}(1-\lambda)^{N_{t}(\widehat{a}_{t})},

and set

P_{t}^{\mathrm{pw}}\leftarrow\big\{E_{t}\geq 3/\varepsilon\big\}
.

2c.

Step(ii): Lower confidence bound on
p_{r}
.

Set
\widehat{p}_{t}(r)\leftarrow N_{t}(r)/t
and

L_{t}=L_{t}(r)\leftarrow\sup\bigl(\{0\}\cup\{q\in(0,\widehat{p}_{t}(r)]:M_{t}(q)\geq 3/\varepsilon\}\bigr).

2d.

Step(iii): Unseen upper bound.

Set
U_{t}\leftarrow\min\{u\in(0,1]:u^{-1}(1-u)^{t}\leq\varepsilon/3\}
.

2e.

If

P_{t}^{\mathrm{pw}}

and

L_{t}>U_{t}

then

return
(\tau\leftarrow t
, “certify
r
as unique mode”).

4.
Theoretical Guarantees

We establish three theoretical guarantees for CITE. First, the false
certification probability is controlled at the prescribed anytime-valid
level for the fixed target
r
(Theorem
4.1
). Second,
under the unique-mode alternative, the expected stopping time is bounded
by a category-set-size-free rate depending on
(\delta,p_{r})
but not on

|\mathcal{A}|
(Theorem
4.3
). Third, a combined
information-theoretic lower bound shows that this rate is optimal up to
constants and logarithmic terms
(Theorem
4.4
).

4.1.
Main Theorems

We first provide the sequential version of Type-I error control on the stopping time
\tau
given in Definition
3.7
.

Theorem 4.1

(Type-I error control)
.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
be a prespecified significance level.
Under Assumption
2.1
, the stopping time
\tau
of
Definition
3.7
satisfies

\sup_{P\in H_{0,r}}\mathbb{P}_{P}(\tau<\infty)\leq\varepsilon.

The proof, deferred to Appendix
B.6
, fixes a
null witness
a^{\star}\neq r
satisfying
p_{a^{\star}}\geq p_{r}
. If this
witness has been observed by the stopping time, runner-up monotonicity
implies that the fixed-competitor e-process

E_{t}^{(r,a^{\star})}
must have crossed
3/\varepsilon
. If
the witness is still unseen, then the inequality
L_{t}>U_{t}
can hold
only if either the LCB or unseen bound has failed. This yields
(
2
) and a union bound over the three

\varepsilon/3
components.

The above result on the Type-I error control does not provide any guarantee regarding the time required to certify the true mode. Under the alternative, write the
modal gap as
\delta:=p_{r}-p_{(2)}>0
. We now show that

\mathbb{E}[\tau]
is bounded by a function of
(\delta,p_{r})
alone,
independent of
|\mathcal{A}|
.

Condition 4.2
.

\Lambda_{\mathrm{pw}}
contains some
\lambda_{\mathrm{pw}}\in[\delta/8,\delta/4]
,
and
\Lambda_{r}
contains some
\lambda_{r}\in[1/32,1/16]
.

Theorem 4.3

(Stopping-time bound)
.

Suppose Assumption
2.1
holds and
Condition
4.2
with
\delta>0
, and the selected grid weights

w_{\lambda_{\mathrm{pw}}},v_{\lambda_{r}}
are bounded below by a
constant independent of
\delta,p_{r},\varepsilon
. Then, we have

\mathbb{P}(\tau<\infty)=1
and

\mathbb{E}[\tau]=O\left(\frac{\log(1/\varepsilon)+\log(1/\delta)}{\delta^{2}}+\frac{\log(1/\varepsilon)+\log(1/p_{r})}{p_{r}}\right),

(7)

with universal constants independent of
\delta
,
p_{r}
,
|\mathcal{A}|
.

We next state the matching lower bound used to interpret
Theorem
4.3
. For
p\in(0,1/2]
and

\delta\in(0,p)
, define

\mathcal{P}(p,\delta):=\left\{P:p_{r}=p,\quad p_{(2)}\leq p-\delta\right\}.

Theorem 4.4

(Combined minimax lower bound)
.

Fix
p\in(0,1/2]
and
\delta\in(0,p)
. Assume either that
\mathcal{A}
is
countably infinite, or that it contains at least
\left\lceil({1-p})/({p-\delta})\right\rceil+2

distinct non-target categories. Let
\tau^{\prime}
be any
level-
\varepsilon
sequential certification procedure for the fixed
target
r
, i.e.,
\sup_{Q\in H_{0,r}}\mathbb{P}_{Q}(\tau^{\prime}<\infty)\leq\varepsilon
.
Assume that, for every
P\in\mathcal{P}(p,\delta)
,
\mathbb{P}_{P}(\tau^{\prime}<\infty)=1
and
\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau^{\prime}]<\infty
hold.
Then, there is a universal constant
c>0
such that

\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}(p,\delta)}\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau^{\prime}]\geq c\log(1/\varepsilon)\left(\frac{p}{\delta^{2}}+\frac{1}{p}\right).

Comparing the lower bound of Theorem
4.4
with the upper bound (
7
), we show that the CITE stopping time achieves the minimax optimal order up to logarithmic factors whenever
p_{r}
is bounded away from zero.

In the very small-
p_{r}
regime, the current upper bound can be worse in the pairwise term; closing this gap is left open.

In Appendix
F
, we give extensions of the analysis: sharper bounds with refined constant (Theorem
F.2
), a growth rate of the e-process
(Theorem
F.8
);
a stopping time bound with an
geometric grid
(Theorem
F.5
); and a top-
k
extension for the Type-I error and the stopping-time bound
(Theorem
F.9
).

5.
Extension to Confidence-Weighted Voting

5.1.
Setup and Weighted CITE

We extend CITE to the confidence-weighted case: suppose we observe a pair
(X_{i},W_{i})
with a
confidence score
W_{i}\in[0,1]
, e.g. the score from logits or a verifier;
see
(
Taubenfeld et al. 2025
;
Fu et al. 2025
)
.
In this case, our purpose is to certify the
weighted
unique mode

\arg\max_{a}\mu_{a}
with
\mu_{a}:=\mathbb{E}[W_{i}\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=a\}]

and weighted modal gap
\Delta_{w}:=\mu_{r}-\mu_{\star}
, where

\mu_{\star}:=\sup_{a\neq r}\mu_{a}
.

Assumption 5.1

(i.i.d. weighted categories)
.

(X_{i},W_{i})_{i\geq 1}
are i.i.d. with
X_{i}\in\mathcal{A}
and
W_{i}\in[0,1]
.

We develop
Weighted CITE
(W-CITE) for this setting. The
weighted null and alternative are

\displaystyle H_{0,r}^{(w)}:=\{P:\mu_{r}\leq\mu_{\star}\},\mbox{
~~and~~}H_{1,r}^{(w)}:=\{P:\mu_{r}>\mu_{\star}\}.

(8)

W-CITE extends the three components of Section
3
to the
weighted masses
(\mu_{a})_{a\in\mathcal{A}}
.

Component (i): Pairwise E-Values.

We consider the weighted indicator

\widetilde{Z}_{i}^{(r,a)}:=W_{i}(\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=r\}-\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=a\})
and a corresponding weighted pairwise e-process and its mixture as

\widetilde{E}_{t}^{(r,a)}:=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{pw}}}w_{\lambda}\widetilde{E}_{t}^{(r,a)}(\lambda),\qquad\widetilde{E}_{t}^{(r,a)}(\lambda):=\prod_{i=1}^{t}\bigl(1+\lambda\widetilde{Z}_{i}^{(r,a)}\bigr).

(9)

Because runner-up monotonicity need not hold with heterogeneous weights,
W-CITE uses the all-competitors check

\min_{a\in\mathcal{A}_{t}\setminus\{r\}}\widetilde{E}_{t}^{(r,a)}\geq 3/\varepsilon
.

Component (ii): LCB on Target Weighted Mass.

The LCB targets
\mu_{r}
via the increment

W_{i}\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=r\}-q
. Let
\widehat{\mu}_{t}(r):=\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=1}^{t}W_{i}\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=r\}
.
For
q\in(0,1]
, define

\widetilde{M}_{t}(q):=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{r}(q)}v_{\lambda}\widetilde{M}_{t}(q,\lambda),\qquad\widetilde{M}_{t}(q,\lambda):=\prod_{i=1}^{t}\bigl(1+\lambda(W_{i}\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=r\}-q)\bigr),

and set

\widetilde{L}_{t}(r):=\sup(\{0\}\cup\{q\in(0,\widehat{\mu}_{t}(r)]:\widetilde{M}_{t}(q)\geq 3/\varepsilon\}).

Component (iii): Unseen Upper Bound.

The unseen bound
U_{t}
is unchanged since
\mu_{a}\leq p_{a}
.

Stopping Time.

W-CITE stops when the weighted pairwise check passes for every observed
competitor and the weighted LCB exceeds the unseen bound,

\widetilde{L}_{t}(r)>U_{t}
; if no non-target competitor has been observed,
the pairwise part is treated as vacuous. This all-competitors check
raises the per-round cost from
O(1)
to
O(|\mathcal{A}_{t}|)
, since runner-up
monotonicity (Lemma
B.3
) fails under heterogeneous
weights. We write
\tau^{(w)}
for the resulting stopping time, and
give the validity analysis in Appendix
D
.

5.2.
Theoretical Guarantee for Weighted CITE

We develop theoretical analysis for W-CITE, including the Type-I error and the stopping time.

Theorem 5.2

(Type-I error, weighted case)
.

Under Assumption
5.1
,

\sup_{P\in H_{0,r}^{(w)}}\mathbb{P}_{P}(\tau^{(w)}<\infty)\leq\varepsilon
.

Condition 5.3

(Weighted grid coverage)
.

\Lambda_{\mathrm{pw}}
contains some

\lambda_{\mathrm{pw}}\in[\Delta_{w}/8,\Delta_{w}/4]
and

\Lambda_{r}
contains some
\lambda_{r}\in[1/32,1/16]
.

Theorem 5.4

(Weighted stopping-time bound)
.

Suppose Assumption
5.1
holds with
\Delta_{w}>0

and Condition
5.3
, and the grid weights

w_{\lambda_{\mathrm{pw}}},v_{\lambda_{r}}
are bounded below by a
constant independent of
\Delta_{w},\mu_{r},\varepsilon
. Then we have

\mathbb{P}(\tau^{(w)}<\infty)=1
and

\mathbb{E}[\tau^{(w)}]=O\left(\frac{\log(1/\varepsilon)+\log(1/\Delta_{w})}{\Delta_{w}^{2}}+\frac{\log(1/\varepsilon)+\log(1/\mu_{r})}{\mu_{r}}\right).

(10)

The weighted guarantee mirrors the unweighted theory (
7
) with

(p_{r},\delta)
replaced by
(\mu_{r},\Delta_{w})
. The bound suggests that weighting
can improve stopping time when it increases the effective gap

\Delta_{w}
without reducing the target weighted mass
\mu_{r}
too much.

6.
Empirical Validation

We evaluate CITE on simulated multinomial distributions and on
LLM self-consistency benchmarks. The experiments are designed to evaluate the following
quantities: empirical false certification (Type-I error) under null
targets, empirical power measured by certification probability under
alternatives, and stopping time or sample efficiency, both on synthetic
multinomial models and on LLM answer distributions. Baselines are
Bonferroni

(fixed-sample union bound),
KR

(
Kim and Ramdas 2025
)

(fixed-target sample-split test), and

MMC

(
Cordero-Encinar and Duncan 2025
)
(sequential
leader-tracking), all at level
\varepsilon=0.05
.
We do not include adaptive stopping methods without theoretical guarantees of Type-I error control.
Detailed protocols, per-setting tables, and ablations on component scaling
and the confidence-weighted W-CITE are deferred to
Appendices
G
and
H
.

Simulations.

We evaluate CITE on five multinomial settings
(Appendix
G.1
). First, we check false
certification under the fixed-target null. In Case B, across all five
settings and all sample sizes, CITE has empirical
false-certification rate zero, consistent with
Theorem
4.1
; the same is true for W-CITE and
fixed-sample Bonferroni. By contrast, KR shows small nonzero
false-certification rates on Settings 3–4, up to
0.002
, and MMC
shows nonzero false certifications in the same regimes, reaching

0.006
on Setting 3 and
0.014
on Setting 4
(Table
3
).

Second, we evaluate certification power and stopping time under the
alternative. In the easy concentrated Setting 2, all methods saturate
quickly: at
N=64
, CITE certifies with rate
0.890
and mean stopping
time
\bar{\tau}\approx 36
, while Bonferroni and KR achieve rates

0.928
and
0.946
, respectively. In the diffuse Setting 1, CITE is
more efficient than Bonferroni before saturation, with certification
rates
0.188
vs.
0.100
at
N=64
and
0.892
vs.
0.804
at

N=128
, reaching full certification by
N=256
with

\bar{\tau}\approx 90
. In the moderate LLM-like Setting 5, CITE reaches
rate
0.544
at
N=256
and
0.932
at
N=512

with
\bar{\tau}\approx 234
, whereas MMC remains at rate at most

0.002
through
N=2048
. KR often has higher finite-sample power, but
it is a fixed-sample baseline rather than an anytime-valid stopping
rule. In the near-tie Setting 3 and very diffuse Setting 4, all
methods remain low-power within the evaluated budget range.

Finally, the component-level simulations are consistent with the two
terms in Theorem
4.3
. When
p_{r}
is varied at
fixed gap, the LCB–unseen component follows the predicted

p_{r}^{-1}
dependence; in the reported sweep,

\bar{\tau}_{\mathrm{lu}}p_{r}
stays between roughly
14
and
20
. When

\delta
is varied at fixed
p_{r}=0.24
, the LCB–unseen component stays
approximately constant, around
\bar{\tau}_{\mathrm{lu}}\approx 70
, while
the pairwise component is the part most affected by the modal gap
(Appendix
G.3
).

LLM self-consistency.

We evaluate the performance of Qwen3-30B
(
Qwen3-30B-A3B-Instruct-2507
)
(
Qwen Team 2025
)
and
gpt-oss-20b
(
openai/gpt-oss-20b
)
(
OpenAI 2025
)
on
AIME 2026
(
Dekoninck et al. 2026
)
,
FrontierScience-Olympiad
(
Wang et al. 2026
)
, and
the clinical benchmark
ER-REASON
(
Mehandru et al. 2025
)
.
Figure
2
plots mean accuracy against the
sample budget; the horizontal axis is the mean stopping time for the
sequential methods (CITE, W-CITE, MMC) and the prefix sample
size
N
for the fixed-sample baselines (Bonferroni, KR).

Figure 2.
Mean accuracy vs. sample budget on Qwen3-30B (top) and
gpt-oss-20b (bottom), across AIME 2026,
FrontierScience-Olympiad, and ER-REASON. For the sequential
methods (CITE, W-CITE, MMC) the horizontal axis is the mean
stopping time; for the fixed-sample baselines (Bonferroni, KR)
it is the prefix sample size
N
.

When the target is set to the empirical runner-up rather than the mode,
the maximum false-certification rate over all evaluated methods, problems,
and budgets is
0.052
on gpt-oss-20b/ER-REASON and at most

0.012
on every other tested (model, dataset) pair; both lie
within Monte Carlo error of the nominal level
\varepsilon=0.05

given the multiplicity of comparisons. On Qwen3-30B/ER-REASON,
fixed-sample Bonferroni saturates at certification rate
0.111

for
N\geq 256
, whereas CITE attains certification rate

\geq 0.98
at mean stopping time
\bar{\tau}\approx 12
. This gives a
direct stopping-time diagnostic: in this concentrated-answer regime,
CITE certifies after roughly a dozen samples on average, while the
fixed-sample baseline remains weak even at much larger budgets. At the largest evaluated budget
N=1024
, the CITE
certification rate exceeds that of MMC by a factor of
approximately
2
on FrontierScience-Olympiad (where the
empirical answer set contains about
250
distinct values per
problem on gpt-oss-20b), while on ER-REASON the two methods
differ by at most
0.054
across all budgets, attained on
gpt-oss-20b. Relative to the KR baseline, the CITE certification
rate is lower by at most
0.087
, attained on
Qwen3-30B/FrontierScience at
N\in\{256,512\}
, while
retaining anytime validity, whereas KR, by construction, is not valid under adaptive stopping.
Per-problem and per-budget tables and the corresponding results
for the W-CITE are deferred to
Appendix
H
.

7.
Conclusion

We introduced CITE for sequentially certifying the
unique mode of a discrete distribution on a countable, possibly
infinite category set. Our theory guarantees that anytime-valid Type-I error control, the stopping time has
the category-set-size free order, and the order matches the information-theoretic lower bound up to logarithmic factors.
We also extend CITE to confidence-weighted voting and develop its weighted version W-CITE. In our experiments, both numerical simulations and real LLM environments demonstrated that CITE achieves high accuracy while controlling Type-I errors.

Limitations and future work.

CITE provides statistical guarantee that a prespecified label is the unique mode of the model’s response distribution. This is logically distinct from, and complementary to,
answer correctness
: the two compose, and neither subsumes the other, so a downstream system can combine our anytime-valid mode certificate with any separate correctness signal. Within this scope, a natural extension is to certify the empirically observed top category
\widehat{r}_{t}\in\arg\max_{a}N_{t}(a)
, i.e., a data-adaptive target; this requires a Bonferroni-type correction over the random sequence of empirical leaders, paralleling the construction of
Cordero-Encinar and Duncan 2025
, and whether the category-size-free rate of Theorem
4.3
survives this correction is left open.

Appendix A
Related Works

Self-consistency and test-time compute for LLM reasoning.

Self-consistency and related inference-time scaling methods improve
LLM reasoning by sampling multiple reasoning trajectories and
aggregating their terminal answers, typically by majority voting

(
Wei et al. 2022
;
Zhou et al. 2023
;
Wang et al. 2023
;
Yao et al. 2023
;
Brown et al. 2024
;
Snell et al. 2025
)
. These methods motivate our
statistical formulation: after normalization, repeated LLM outputs are
viewed as i.i.d. draws from an unknown categorical distribution over
terminal response labels. Our work is not a new prompting, search, or
reranking method. Instead, it studies when one may stop sampling and
certify, with a prescribed error level, that a prespecified response
label is the unique mode of this distribution. This is a guarantee
about the model-induced response distribution, and is distinct from
semantic correctness of the answer.

Adaptive self-consistency and early stopping.

A growing line of work reduces the cost of self-consistency by adapting
the number of sampled trajectories to the apparent difficulty of each
query. Adaptive-Consistency uses posterior agreement among sampled
answers as a lightweight stopping signal
(
Aggarwal et al. 2023
)
;
Early-Stopping Self-Consistency stops when a low-entropy window
indicates sufficient agreement
(
Li et al. 2024
)
; difficulty-adaptive
and dynamic variants allocate inference budgets using difficulty
estimates, answer-distribution uncertainty, or reasoning-path quality

(
Wang et al. 2025b
;
Wang et al. 2025a
)
; and confidence-aware methods
use response-level confidence or verifier-like scores to improve
aggregation and stopping

(
Taubenfeld et al. 2025
;
Aghazadeh et al. 2025
;
Fu et al. 2025
)
. These
methods are primarily designed to improve the accuracy–cost tradeoff.
By contrast, CITE is designed for statistical certification: it controls
the probability of falsely certifying the target as the unique mode
under arbitrary data-driven stopping, at a user-specified level

\varepsilon
.

Certified self-consistency.

The closest LLM-specific statistical work is the Martingale Majority
Certificate (MMC) of
Cordero-Encinar and Duncan 2025
, which develops
finite-sample and anytime-valid certificates for self-consistency and
uses martingale arguments to determine when enough samples have been
drawn.
MMC provides an important first step toward certified self-consistency, but it certifies a stronger majority/residual-tail condition than the fixed-target unique-mode property considered here.
Our target differs in two respects. First, CITE tests a
fixed-target unique-mode alternative,

p_{r}>\sup_{a\neq r}p_{a}
, whereas MMC-style certificates are naturally
tied to a stronger majority or residual-tail domination condition.
These two notions coincide only in non-diffuse regimes. In LLM
self-consistency, however, the modal answer may have substantially
larger mass than every individual competitor while still being smaller
than the aggregate mass of the long tail.
Proposition
E.1
shows that on a provable least-favourable family in this regime, the MMC certifies with probability at most the nominal level
\varepsilon
.
Second, CITE explicitly
handles the possibility that a high-mass competitor has not yet been
observed. This unseen-category component is essential when the set of
normalized free-form answers is unknown and grows during sampling.

Anytime-valid inference, e-values, and confidence sequences.

Our construction builds on the classical martingale view of sequential
testing
(
Ville 1939
)
and the modern theory of e-values,
e-processes, testing by betting, and confidence sequences

(
Howard et al. 2020
;
Howard et al. 2021
;
Shafer 2021
;
Vovk and Wang 2021
;
Lindon and Malek 2022
;
Grünwald et al. 2024
;
Ramdas and Wang 2025
)
. These
tools provide tests and confidence statements that remain valid under
continuous monitoring and data-dependent stopping. CITE instantiates
this machinery for a composite categorical unique-mode problem: it
combines pairwise e-processes against observed competitors with a
time-uniform lower confidence bound for the target mass and an upper
bound for unseen competitors. The resulting intersection-union test
yields a certification rule whose Type-I error is valid uniformly over
all stopping times.

Best-arm identification and discrete argmin inference.

The fixed-target unique-mode problem is related to fixed-confidence
best-arm identification and pure-exploration bandits

(
Garivier and Kaufmann 2016
;
Kaufmann et al. 2016
;
Kaufmann and Koolen 2021
)
.
Those works characterize the sample complexity of identifying the best
arm under sequential sampling and often rely on a known finite set of
arms. Our setting is different: each LLM query returns a single
categorical draw from the entire response distribution, the category set
is not known in advance, and a previously unseen response can become a
relevant competitor. The closest fixed-target finite-dimensional
inference problem is the dimension-agnostic discrete argmin test of

Kim and Ramdas 2025
, which we include as a baseline. CITE differs by
providing an anytime-valid stopping rule for a countable and unknown
category set, rather than a fixed-sample or sample-split test over a
given finite vector.

Unseen species, missing mass, and support-agnostic inference.

The need to reason about unobserved response labels connects our work to
the classical unseen-species and missing-mass literature, including
Good–Turing and Good–Toulmin estimation

(
Good 1953
;
Good and Toulmin 1956
)
, concentration bounds for
the missing mass

(
McAllester and Ortiz 2003
;
Berend and Kontorovich 2013
)
,
and minimax prediction of unseen species

(
Orlitsky et al. 2016
)
. These works estimate or bound quantities
such as the aggregate probability of unseen outcomes or the number of
new species. CITE uses a different object: for certification of a
unique mode, it is enough to rule out the existence of a single unseen
competitor whose mass could exceed the target. The unseen component of
CITE is therefore tailored to sequential modal certification rather than
to estimating the entire missing mass or support size.

Confidence-weighted aggregation.

Recent work shows that response-level confidence can improve
self-consistency by weighting or prioritizing high-confidence reasoning
paths
(
Taubenfeld et al. 2025
;
Aghazadeh et al. 2025
;
Fu et al. 2025
)
.
Our weighted extension formalizes a complementary question: when the
observations are pairs
(X_{i},W_{i})
with bounded confidence weights, can
one certify that a target label is the unique weighted mode? W-CITE
extends the same anytime-valid and category-agnostic guarantee to this
weighted setting, with the weighted gap replacing the unweighted modal
gap in the stopping-time analysis.

Appendix B
Proofs for Section
3

Let
\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}},\alpha_{r},\alpha_{u}>0
be error levels of each component satisfying

\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}+\alpha_{r}+\alpha_{u}\leq\varepsilon
.
The main text uses the equal split

\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}=\alpha_{r}=\alpha_{u}=\varepsilon/3.

For readability, the Appendix proofs keep the symbols

\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}},\alpha_{r},\alpha_{u}
and substitute the equal split
only at the end.

We prepare some supportive results as follow.

For an
(\mathcal{F}_{t})
-adapted nonnegative process
(M_{t})_{t\geq 0}

with
\mathbb{E}_{P}[M_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]\leq M_{t-1}
and
M_{0}\leq 1

(an NSM), Ville’s inequality
(
Ville 1939
)
gives the
time-uniform tail bound

P\Bigl(\sup_{t\geq 0}M_{t}\geq 1/\alpha\Bigr)\leq\alpha,\qquad\alpha\in(0,1],

(11)

which we invoke repeatedly in what follows.

Lemma B.1

(Attainment of a positive supremum)
.

Let
(x_{a})_{a\in\mathcal{B}}
be a nonnegative summable sequence indexed by a
countable set
\mathcal{B}
. If
\sup_{a\in\mathcal{B}}x_{a}>0
, then the supremum is
attained.

Proof.

Let
s:=\sup_{a\in\mathcal{B}}x_{a}>0
. If no index attained
s
, then for each

n
one could choose a distinct
a_{n}\in\mathcal{B}
with
x_{a_{n}}>s/2
. This
would contradict
\sum_{a\in\mathcal{B}}x_{a}<\infty
.
∎

Lemma B.2

(Pairwise NSM)
.

Fix
a\neq r
and
\lambda\in(0,1)
. Under any
P
with

p_{r}\leq p_{a}
, the process

(E_{t}^{(r,a)}(\lambda))_{t\geq 0}
is an NSM with

E_{0}^{(r,a)}(\lambda)=1
.

Lemma B.3

(Runner-up monotonicity)
.

For any
a,b\in\mathcal{A}_{t}\setminus\{r\}
with

N_{t}(a)\geq N_{t}(b)
and any
\lambda\in(0,1)
, it holds that

E_{t}^{(r,a)}(\lambda)\leq E_{t}^{(r,b)}(\lambda).

Consequently, we have

E_{t}^{(r,a)}\leq E_{t}^{(r,b)}.

B.1.
Proof of Lemma
B.2

Proof.

We have
E_{0}=1
(empty product) and
E_{t}\geq 0
since each factor

1+\lambda Z_{i}\geq 1-\lambda>0
. For the supermartingale
property:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[E_{t}^{(r,a)}(\lambda)\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]

\displaystyle=E_{t-1}^{(r,a)}(\lambda)\cdot(1+\lambda\mathbb{E}[Z_{t}^{(r,a)}])

\displaystyle=E_{t-1}^{(r,a)}(\lambda)\cdot(1+\lambda(p_{r}-p_{a})),

using the i.i.d. property. Under
p_{r}\leq p_{a}
, the factor

1+\lambda(p_{r}-p_{a})\leq 1
.
∎

B.2.
Proof of Corollary
3.2

Proof.

For each
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{pw}}
,

(E_{t}^{(r,a)}(\lambda))_{t\geq 0}
is an NSM under
p_{r}\leq p_{a}

by Lemma
B.2
. Since

{E}_{t}^{(r,a)}=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{pw}}}w_{\lambda}E_{t}^{(r,a)}(\lambda)

is a nonnegative weighted sum with total weight at most one, it is also
an NSM and satisfies
E_{0}^{(r,a)}\leq 1
. Ville’s inequality
(
11
) then gives

\mathbb{P}_{P}\left(\sup_{t\geq 0}E_{t}^{(r,a)}\geq\alpha^{-1}\right)\leq\alpha.

∎

B.3.
Proof of Lemma
B.3

Proof.

By (
3
),

E_{t}^{(r,a)}(\lambda)=(1+\lambda)^{N_{t}(r)}(1-\lambda)^{N_{t}(a)}
.
The factor
(1+\lambda)^{N_{t}(r)}
depends only on
r
and is common to both

E_{t}^{(r,a)}(\lambda)
and
E_{t}^{(r,b)}(\lambda)
.
Since
0<1-\lambda<1
and
N_{t}(a)\geq N_{t}(b)
,

(1-\lambda)^{N_{t}(a)}\leq(1-\lambda)^{N_{t}(b)},

whence
E_{t}^{(r,a)}(\lambda)\leq E_{t}^{(r,b)}(\lambda)
. Multiplying
by the nonnegative weights
w_{\lambda}
and summing over

\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{pw}}
gives

E_{t}^{(r,a)}\leq E_{t}^{(r,b)}.

∎

B.4.
Proof of Proposition
3.4

Proof.

If
p_{r}=0
, then
N_{t}(r)=0
a.s. for all
t
, so
\widehat{p}_{t}(r)=0

and
L_{t}(r)=0=p_{r}
a.s.;
the event
\{\exists t:L_{t}(r)>p_{r}\}
has probability
0
.
Assume
p_{r}>0
in the remainder.

Step 1: Monotonicity in
q
.

For any
0<q_{1}<q_{2}\leq 1
,
\Lambda_{r}(q_{2})\subseteq\Lambda_{r}(q_{1})

and each factor

1+\lambda(\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=r\}-q)
is nonincreasing in
q
; hence

q\mapsto M_{t}(q)
is nonincreasing.

Step 2: NSM at
q=p_{r}
.

Set
M_{t}(q,\lambda):=\prod_{i=1}^{t}(1+\lambda(\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=r\}-q))
.
For
\lambda\in\Lambda_{r}(p_{r})
, every factor

1+\lambda(\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=r\}-p_{r})\geq 1-\lambda p_{r}>0
,
and
\mathbb{E}[1+\lambda(\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=r\}-p_{r})]=1
, so

(M_{t}(p_{r},\lambda))_{t\geq 0}
is a nonnegative martingale with
initial value
1
. The mixture

M_{t}(p_{r}):=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{r}(p_{r})}v_{\lambda}M_{t}(p_{r},\lambda)

is therefore an NSM with

M_{0}(p_{r})=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{r}(p_{r})}v_{\lambda}\leq 1

(Corollary-style argument analogous to Corollary
3.2
).
Ville’s inequality (
11
) gives

\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\sup_{t\geq 0}M_{t}(p_{r})\geq 1/\alpha_{r}\Bigr)\leq\alpha_{r}.

(12)

Step 3: Event inclusion.

Suppose
L_{t}(r)>p_{r}
for some
t\geq 1
. By the definition of

L_{t}(r)
, there exists
q\in(p_{r},\widehat{p}_{t}(r)]
with

M_{t}(q)\geq 1/\alpha_{r}
. Step 1 then implies

M_{t}(p_{r})\geq M_{t}(q)\geq 1/\alpha_{r}
. Hence

\{\exists t\geq 1:L_{t}(r)>p_{r}\}\subseteq\{\sup_{t\geq 0}M_{t}(p_{r})\geq 1/\alpha_{r}\},

and the desired bound follows from (
12
).
∎

B.5.
Proof of Proposition
3.6

Proof.

For a fixed integer
t\geq 1
, define

f_{t}(u):=u^{-1}(1-u)^{t}
for
u\in(0,1]
.
Since
(\log f_{t})^{\prime}(u)=-1/u-t/(1-u)<0
,

f_{t}
is strictly decreasing on
(0,1]
.

Now fix
a\in\mathcal{A}
with
p_{a}>0
.
Since
U_{t}
is nonincreasing in
t
, define

t_{*}(a):=\inf\{t\geq 1:p_{a}>U_{t}\}
(possibly
+\infty
).
If
t_{*}(a)=\infty
, then
p_{a}\leq U_{t}
for all
t
, so the event

\{\exists t:N_{t}(a)=0,p_{a}>U_{t}\}
is empty.
Otherwise,
t_{*}(a)<\infty
and
p_{a}>U_{t_{*}(a)}
.
For any
t\geq t_{*}(a)
with
p_{a}>U_{t}
, we have
N_{t}(a)=0
only if

N_{t_{*}(a)}(a)=0
(since counts are nondecreasing), so the event

\{\exists t:N_{t}(a)=0,p_{a}>U_{t}\}
is contained in
\{N_{t_{*}(a)}(a)=0\}
.
By definition of
U_{t_{*}(a)}
, we have

f_{t_{*}(a)}(U_{t_{*}(a)})\leq\alpha_{u}
.
Since
p_{a}>U_{t_{*}(a)}
and
f_{t_{*}(a)}
is strictly decreasing,

f_{t_{*}(a)}(p_{a})=p_{a}^{-1}(1-p_{a})^{t_{*}(a)}<f_{t_{*}(a)}(U_{t_{*}(a)})\leq\alpha_{u}
, hence

\mathbb{P}\bigl(N_{t_{*}(a)}(a)=0\bigr)=(1-p_{a})^{t_{*}(a)}<\alpha_{u}p_{a}.

A probability-weighted union bound over the countable category set gives

\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\exists a\in\mathcal{A},\exists t\geq 1:N_{t}(a)=0\text{ and }p_{a}>U_{t}\Bigr)\leq\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\alpha_{u}p_{a}=\alpha_{u}.\qed

B.6.
Proof of Theorem
4.1

Proof.

Fix any
P\in H_{0,r}
. We choose a deterministic

a^{\star}\neq r
such that

p_{a^{\star}}\geq p_{r}.

We decompose the Type-I event according to whether
a^{\star}
has been
observed by time
\tau
:

\{\tau<\infty\}\subseteq\underbrace{\{\tau<\infty,\ a^{\star}\in\mathcal{A}_{\tau}\}}_{E_{1}}\cup\underbrace{\{\tau<\infty,\ a^{\star}\notin\mathcal{A}_{\tau}\}}_{E_{2}}.

(13)

Step 1 (bounding
\mathbb{P}(E_{1})
).

On
E_{1}
, the witness
a^{\star}
is observed at time
\tau
, so

\mathcal{A}_{\tau}\setminus\{r\}\neq\emptyset
. Since CITE stops at
\tau
, the
pairwise part of the stopping rule gives

E_{\tau}^{(r,\widehat{a}_{\tau})}=E_{\tau}\geq\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}^{-1}.

By the definition of the empirical runner-up,

N_{\tau}(\widehat{a}_{\tau})\geq N_{\tau}(a^{\star})
. Lemma
B.3

then gives

E_{\tau}^{(r,\widehat{a}_{\tau})}\leq E_{\tau}^{(r,a^{\star})}.

Thus

E_{\tau}^{(r,a^{\star})}\geq\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}^{-1}.

Since
a^{\star}
is deterministic under
P
and

p_{r}\leq p_{a^{\star}}
, Corollary
3.2
and Ville’s
inequality yield

\mathbb{P}_{P}(E_{1})\leq\mathbb{P}_{P}\left(\sup_{t\geq 0}E_{t}^{(r,a^{\star})}\geq\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}^{-1}\right)\leq\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}.

(14)

Step 2 (bounding
\mathbb{P}(E_{2})
).

On
E_{2}
, CITE stops at
\tau
, so
L_{\tau}(r)>U_{\tau}
, and

a^{\star}
is unseen at
\tau
. Define the two failure events

\displaystyle F_{r}

\displaystyle:=\{\exists t\geq 1:L_{t}(r)>p_{r}\},

\displaystyle F_{u}

\displaystyle:=\{\exists a\in\mathcal{A},\ \exists t\geq 1:N_{t}(a)=0\text{ and }p_{a}>U_{t}\}.

Propositions
3.4
and
3.6
give

\mathbb{P}_{P}(F_{r})\leq\alpha_{r}
and
\mathbb{P}_{P}(F_{u})\leq\alpha_{u}
.
On
E_{2}\setminus(F_{r}\cup F_{u})
, we have

L_{\tau}(r)\leq p_{r}
and, because
a^{\star}
is unseen at
\tau
,

p_{a^{\star}}\leq U_{\tau}
. Combining these inequalities with

L_{\tau}(r)>U_{\tau}
yields

p_{a^{\star}}\leq U_{\tau}<L_{\tau}(r)\leq p_{r},

which contradicts
p_{a^{\star}}\geq p_{r}
. Hence,

E_{2}\subseteq F_{r}\cup F_{u}
, and

\mathbb{P}_{P}(E_{2})\leq\mathbb{P}_{P}(F_{r})+\mathbb{P}_{P}(F_{u})\leq\alpha_{r}+\alpha_{u}.

(15)

Step 3 (union bound).

Combining (
14
)–(
15
),

\mathbb{P}_{P}(\tau<\infty)\leq\mathbb{P}_{P}(E_{1})+\mathbb{P}_{P}(E_{2})\leq\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}+\alpha_{r}+\alpha_{u}\leq\varepsilon.\qed

Appendix C
Power Analysis for CITE

Throughout this section,
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots
are i.i.d. on the countable
category set
\mathcal{A}
, and the fixed target
r
is the unique mode. Write

p_{(2)}:=\sup_{a\neq r}p_{a},\qquad\delta:=p_{r}-p_{(2)}>0.

For the analysis, define the conservative competitor count

R_{t}:=\max_{a\neq r}N_{t}(a),

where zero-count competitors are included in the maximum, and define

E_{t}^{\circ}:=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{pw}}}w_{\lambda}(1+\lambda)^{N_{t}(r)}(1-\lambda)^{R_{t}}.

The corresponding conservative stopping time is

\tau^{\circ}:=\inf\left\{t\geq 1:E_{t}^{\circ}\geq\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}^{-1}\text{ and }L_{t}(r)>U_{t}\right\}.

This stopping time is no earlier than the implemented CITE stopping
time: replacing the observed runner-up count by

R_{t}=\max_{a\neq r}N_{t}(a)
can only make the pairwise condition harder,
while the LCB–unseen condition is unchanged. Hence

\tau\leq\tau^{\circ}\quad\text{pathwise},

and it suffices to upper-bound
\mathbb{E}[\tau^{\circ}]
.

C.1.
Auxiliary lemmas for the clean stopping-time bound

Lemma C.1

(A deterministic logarithmic inequality)
.

Let
A>0
,
C>0
, and
t>0
. If

t\geq 2A\log\bigl((AC)\vee e\bigr),

then

t\geq A\log(Ct).

Proof.

Set
u=t/A
. The assumption gives

u\geq 2\log((AC)\vee e)\geq 2
, so

\log(CA)\leq u/2
and
\log u\leq u/2
. Therefore

\log(Ct)=\log(CA)+\log u\leq u=t/A.

∎

Lemma C.2

(Expected maximum competitor count)
.

For every integer
t\geq 1
,

\mathbb{E}[R_{t}]\leq tp_{(2)}+2\sqrt{2t\log(t+1)}.

Proof.

For
a\neq r
, set
f_{a}(x):=\mathbf{1}\{x=a\}
. Since

p_{a}\leq p_{(2)}
,

\mathbb{E}[R_{t}]-tp_{(2)}\leq\mathbb{E}\sup_{a\neq r}\sum_{i=1}^{t}\{f_{a}(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}f_{a}(X_{i})\}.

By symmetrization, the right-hand side is at most

2\mathbb{E}\mathbb{E}_{\sigma}\sup_{a\neq r}\sum_{i=1}^{t}\sigma_{i}f_{a}(X_{i}),

where
\sigma_{i}
are i.i.d. Rademacher variables independent of the
sample. Conditional on the sample, the vectors

(f_{a}(X_{1}),\ldots,f_{a}(X_{t})),\qquad a\neq r,

have disjoint supports and hence at most
t+1
distinct values, each
with Euclidean norm at most
\sqrt{t}
. The standard exponential
maximal inequality gives

\mathbb{E}_{\sigma}\sup_{a\neq r}\sum_{i=1}^{t}\sigma_{i}f_{a}(X_{i})\leq\sqrt{2t\log(t+1)}.

Combining the displays proves the claim.
∎

Lemma C.3

(LCB monotonicity and inversion)
.

Fix
t\geq 1
. For

M_{t}(q,\lambda):=\prod_{i=1}^{t}\bigl\{1+\lambda(\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=r\}-q)\bigr\},

the following hold.

(a)

The map
q\mapsto M_{t}(q)
is nonincreasing on
(0,1]
.

(b)

If
M_{t}(q)>1
, then
\widehat{p}_{t}(r)>q
.

(c)

If
M_{t}(q)\geq\alpha_{r}^{-1}
, then
L_{t}(r)\geq q
.

Proof.

For (a), if
q_{1}<q_{2}
, then

\Lambda_{r}(q_{2})\subseteq\Lambda_{r}(q_{1})
, and each remaining factor

1+\lambda(\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=r\}-q)
is nonincreasing in
q
. Hence

M_{t}(q_{1})\geq M_{t}(q_{2})
.

For (b), suppose
\widehat{p}_{t}(r)\leq q
. For fixed

\lambda\in\Lambda_{r}(q)
, write

\phi(\lambda):=\widehat{p}_{t}(r)\log(1+\lambda(1-q))+(1-\widehat{p}_{t}(r))\log(1-\lambda q).

Then
\log M_{t}(q,\lambda)=t\phi(\lambda)
,

\phi(0)=0
,
\phi^{\prime}(0)=\widehat{p}_{t}(r)-q\leq 0
, and
\phi
is concave on

[0,1/q)
. Therefore
M_{t}(q,\lambda)\leq 1
for every admissible

\lambda
, so

M_{t}(q)\leq\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{r}(q)}v_{\lambda}\leq 1.

This proves the contrapositive.

For (c), if
M_{t}(q)\geq\alpha_{r}^{-1}>1
, then (b) gives

\widehat{p}_{t}(r)>q
, so
q
belongs to the set defining
L_{t}(r)
.
Hence
L_{t}(r)\geq q
.
∎

Lemma C.4

(Quadratic lower bound for
\log(1+x)
)
.

For every
x\in[-1/2,1/2]
,

\log(1+x)\geq x-x^{2}.

Proof.

Let
h(x):=\log(1+x)-x+x^{2}
. Then
h(0)=0
and

h^{\prime}(x)=x(1+2x)/(1+x)
, which is nonpositive on
[-1/2,0]
and
nonnegative on
[0,1/2]
. Thus
h(x)\geq 0
on
[-1/2,1/2]
.
∎

Proposition C.5

(Small-bet pairwise drift)
.

Assume
\delta>0
and

0<\lambda\leq\frac{\delta}{2(p_{r}+p_{(2)})}.

Then, for every
a\neq r
with
p_{a}\leq p_{(2)}
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\log\{1+\lambda(\mathbf{1}\{X=r\}-\mathbf{1}\{X=a\})\}\right]\geq\frac{\lambda\delta}{2}.

Proof.

Since
\delta\leq p_{r}+p_{(2)}
, the assumed bound gives

\lambda\leq 1/2
. Let

\xi:=\lambda(\mathbf{1}\{X=r\}-\mathbf{1}\{X=a\})\in[-1/2,1/2].

By Lemma
C.4
,

\mathbb{E}[\log(1+\xi)]\geq\lambda(p_{r}-p_{a})-\lambda^{2}(p_{r}+p_{a}).

Using
p_{a}\leq p_{(2)}
gives

\mathbb{E}[\log(1+\xi)]\geq\lambda\delta-\lambda^{2}(p_{r}+p_{(2)})\geq\lambda\delta/2.

∎

C.2.
Proof of Theorem
4.3

Proof.

We prove the bound for
\tau^{\circ}
; since
\tau\leq\tau^{\circ}

pathwise, the same expectation bound holds for the implemented CITE
stopping time.

Step 1: pairwise condition.

Let
\lambda:=\lambda_{\mathrm{pw}}\in[\delta/8,\delta/4]
be the grid
point from Condition
4.2
, with weight
w_{\lambda}
, and set

B_{\mathrm{pw}}:=\log\{1/(\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}w_{\lambda})\}.

For this fixed
\lambda
, define

S_{t}:=N_{t}(r)\log(1+\lambda)+R_{t}\log(1-\lambda).

Since
E_{t}^{\circ}\geq w_{\lambda}\exp(S_{t})
, the pairwise condition

E_{t}^{\circ}\geq\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}^{-1}
holds whenever

S_{t}\geq B_{\mathrm{pw}}
.

Using Lemma
C.2
and
\log(1-\lambda)<0
,

\mathbb{E}[S_{t}]\geq t\{p_{r}\log(1+\lambda)+p_{(2)}\log(1-\lambda)\}-C\lambda\sqrt{t\log(t+1)}.

Because
p_{r}+p_{(2)}\leq 1
,
\lambda\leq\delta/4
, and

\log(1+x)\geq x-x^{2}
on
[-1/2,1/2]
,

p_{r}\log(1+\lambda)+p_{(2)}\log(1-\lambda)\geq\lambda\delta-\lambda^{2}(p_{r}+p_{(2)})\geq c\delta^{2}

for a universal constant
c>0
. Hence

\mathbb{E}[S_{t}]\geq c_{0}\delta^{2}t-C_{0}\delta\sqrt{t\log(t+1)}.

Changing one observation changes both
N_{t}(r)
and
R_{t}
by at most one,
so
S_{t}
is
C_{1}\delta
-Lipschitz in each sample. McDiarmid’s
inequality gives

\mathbb{P}\{S_{t}<\mathbb{E}[S_{t}]-x\}\leq\exp\left\{-\frac{c_{1}x^{2}}{t\delta^{2}}\right\}.

Taking
x=C_{2}\delta\sqrt{t\log(et)}
and using
Lemma
C.1
, we obtain, for a universal constant
C
,

\mathbb{P}\left(E_{t}^{\circ}<\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}^{-1}\right)\leq(et)^{-2},\qquad t\geq T_{\mathrm{pw}}:=C\frac{B_{\mathrm{pw}}+\log(1/\delta)}{\delta^{2}}.

(16)

Step 2: LCB–unseen condition.

Set
q_{0}:=p_{r}/2
. Condition
4.2
gives

\lambda_{r}\in[1/32,1/16]\subset\Lambda_{r}(q_{0})
with weight

v_{\lambda_{r}}
. The unseen bound satisfies
U_{t}\leq p_{r}/4
once

t\geq Cp_{r}^{-1}\log\{1/(\alpha_{u}p_{r})\}.

Let

A_{t}:=\left\{\frac{3}{4}p_{r}t\leq N_{t}(r)\leq\frac{5}{4}p_{r}t\right\}.

Bernstein’s inequality gives

\mathbb{P}(A_{t}^{c})\leq 2\exp(-c_{2}p_{r}t).

On
A_{t}
, the single LCB factor

M_{t}(q_{0},\lambda_{r})=\prod_{i=1}^{t}\{1+\lambda_{r}(\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=r\}-q_{0})\}

satisfies

\log M_{t}(q_{0},\lambda_{r})\geq c_{3}p_{r}t

for a universal constant
c_{3}>0
. Therefore

\widetilde{M}_{t}(q_{0})\geq v_{\lambda_{r}}M_{t}(q_{0},\lambda_{r})\geq\alpha_{r}^{-1}

whenever

t\geq Cp_{r}^{-1}\log\{1/(\alpha_{r}v_{\lambda_{r}})\}.

By Lemma
C.3
, this implies

L_{t}(r)\geq q_{0}>U_{t}
. Combining this with the Bernstein tail and
Lemma
C.1
, we obtain

\mathbb{P}\{L_{t}(r)\leq U_{t}\}\leq(et)^{-2}

(17)

for all

t\geq T_{\mathrm{rare}}:=C\frac{\log(1/\alpha_{r})+\log(1/\alpha_{u})+\log(1/v_{\lambda_{r}})+\log(1/p_{r})}{p_{r}}.

Step 3: combine.

Let

T_{\mathrm{cl}}:=\max\{T_{\mathrm{pw}},T_{\mathrm{rare}}\}.

For all
t\geq T_{\mathrm{cl}}
,

\mathbb{P}(\tau^{\circ}>t)\leq 2(et)^{-2}.

The tail-sum identity gives

\mathbb{E}[\tau^{\circ}]\leq T_{\mathrm{cl}}+1.

Finally, substituting

\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}=\alpha_{r}=\alpha_{u}=\varepsilon/3
and using the
assumption that
w_{\lambda}
and
v_{\lambda_{r}}
are bounded below by
constants,

\mathbb{E}[\tau]\leq\mathbb{E}[\tau^{\circ}]=O\left(\frac{\log(1/\varepsilon)+\log(1/\delta)}{\delta^{2}}+\frac{\log(1/\varepsilon)+\log(1/p_{r})}{p_{r}}\right),

with constants independent of
\delta,p_{r}
, and
|\mathcal{A}|
.
∎

C.3.
Oracle pairwise growth rate

Proposition C.6

(Closed form for the oracle pairwise growth rate)
.

For
\lambda\in(0,1)
, define

\rho(\lambda):=p_{r}\log(1+\lambda)+p_{(2)}\log(1-\lambda).

Then
\rho
is strictly concave on
(0,1)
. If
p_{(2)}>0
, its unique
maximizer is

\lambda^{\star}=\frac{p_{r}-p_{(2)}}{p_{r}+p_{(2)}}=\frac{\delta}{p_{r}+p_{(2)}},

and the maximal value is

\rho^{\star}:=\rho(\lambda^{\star})=p_{r}\log\frac{2p_{r}}{p_{r}+p_{(2)}}+p_{(2)}\log\frac{2p_{(2)}}{p_{r}+p_{(2)}}.

If
p_{(2)}=0
, then
\rho(\lambda)=p_{r}\log(1+\lambda)
is increasing
on
(0,1)
and

\sup_{0<\lambda<1}\rho(\lambda)=p_{r}\log 2.

Proof.

We have

\rho^{\prime}(\lambda)=\frac{p_{r}}{1+\lambda}-\frac{p_{(2)}}{1-\lambda},\qquad\rho^{\prime\prime}(\lambda)=-\frac{p_{r}}{(1+\lambda)^{2}}-\frac{p_{(2)}}{(1-\lambda)^{2}}<0.

Thus
\rho
is strictly concave. When
p_{(2)}>0
, solving

\rho^{\prime}(\lambda)=0
gives

\lambda^{\star}=(p_{r}-p_{(2)})/(p_{r}+p_{(2)})
, and substituting this value
gives the stated formula for
\rho^{\star}
. The case
p_{(2)}=0
is
immediate.
∎

Lemma C.7

(Stopped KL identity for the swapped alternative)
.

Assume
p_{(2)}>0
. Fix

a^{\star}\in\arg\max_{a\neq r}p_{a},

and let
Q
be obtained from
P
by swapping the masses of
r
and

a^{\star}
:

q_{r}=p_{(2)},\qquad q_{a^{\star}}=p_{r},\qquad q_{a}=p_{a}\quad\text{for }a\notin\{r,a^{\star}\}.

Let
P^{\tau}
and
Q^{\tau}
denote the laws of the stopped experiment

(\tau,X_{1},\dots,X_{\tau})
under
P
and
Q
. If

\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau]<\infty
, then

D(P^{\tau}\|Q^{\tau})=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau]\delta\log\frac{p_{r}}{p_{(2)}}.

Proof.

Let
\ell(x):=\log(P(x)/Q(x))
. Since
\ell
is bounded
(both
P
and
Q
have positive masses on the same support), the
stopping factor cancels in the likelihood ratio

dP^{\tau}/dQ^{\tau}=\prod_{i\leq\tau}(P(X_{i})/Q(X_{i}))
, and Wald’s
identity for i.i.d. bounded summands yields

D(P^{\tau}\|Q^{\tau})=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{\tau}\ell(X_{i})\right]=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau]\mathbb{E}_{P}[\ell(X_{1})].

A direct calculation gives

\mathbb{E}_{P}[\ell(X_{1})]=p_{r}\log(p_{r}/p_{(2)})+p_{(2)}\log(p_{(2)}/p_{r})=\delta\log(p_{r}/p_{(2)})
, completing the proof.
∎

Corollary C.8

(Information-theoretic lower bound)
.

Assume
p_{(2)}>0
. Let
\varepsilon
be the Type-I error level of a sequential test for

H_{0,r}
, and assume that under the alternative
P
one has

\mathbb{P}_{P}(\tau<\infty)=1\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau]<\infty.

Then

\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau]\geq\frac{\log(1/\varepsilon)}{\delta\log(p_{r}/p_{(2)})}.

In particular, if
\delta/p_{r}\to 0
, then

\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau]\geq\frac{p_{r}\log(1/\varepsilon)}{\delta^{2}}\bigl(1+o(1)\bigr).

Proof.

Let
Q
be the swap from Lemma
C.7
, so

Q\in H_{0,r}
and Type-I validity gives

Q(\tau<\infty)\leq\varepsilon
. Set
A:=\{\tau<\infty\}
, so

P(A)=1
. By data processing followed by
Lemma
C.7
,

\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\leq\mathrm{kl}\bigl(P(A),Q(A)\bigr)\leq D(P^{\tau}\|Q^{\tau})=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau]\delta\log(p_{r}/p_{(2)}),

which is the first claim. For the small-gap expansion,

\delta\log(p_{r}/p_{(2)})=\delta\log(1+\delta/p_{(2)})=\delta^{2}/p_{r}+o(\delta^{2}/p_{r})
as
\delta/p_{r}\to 0
.
∎

Lemma C.9

(Forward I-projection of
P
onto
H_{0,r}
)
.

Assume
p_{(2)}>0
and pick any
a^{\star}\in\arg\max_{a\neq r}p_{a}

(this set may have ties). Every forward I-projection

P^{\star}\in\arg\min_{Q\in H_{0,r}}D(P\|Q)

arises by midpointing
r
with one of the strongest competitors; for
the choice
a^{\star}
above, the corresponding projection is the

midpoint distribution

p_{r}^{\star}=p_{a^{\star}}^{\star}=\frac{p_{r}+p_{(2)}}{2},\qquad p_{a}^{\star}=p_{a}\quad\text{for }a\notin\{r,a^{\star}\},

and the per-sample KL divergence is

D(P\|P^{\star})=p_{r}\log\frac{2p_{r}}{p_{r}+p_{(2)}}+p_{(2)}\log\frac{2p_{(2)}}{p_{r}+p_{(2)}}.

In particular, as
\delta/p_{r}\to 0
,

D(P\|P^{\star})=\frac{\delta^{2}}{4p_{r}}\bigl(1+o(1)\bigr).

Proof.

H_{0,r}=\bigcup_{a\neq r}\{q_{r}\leq q_{a}\}
is a (possibly countable)
union of half-spaces, hence not itself convex. We minimise

D(P\|\cdot)
over each face
\{q_{r}\leq q_{a}\}
separately (on each
face
D(P\|\cdot)
is strictly convex and strictly positive since

P\notin H_{0,r}
), and then take the smallest of the per-face
minima. The optimum on each face lies on its boundary

\{q_{r}=q_{a}\}
. We organise the proof in three steps.

Step 1 (per-pair midpoint projection).

For each
a\neq r
with
p_{a}<p_{r}
, the I-projection of
P
onto
the affine subspace
\{q_{r}=q_{a}\}
is given in closed form by

q_{r}=q_{a}=\frac{p_{r}+p_{a}}{2},\qquad q_{b}=p_{b}\quad\text{for }b\notin\{r,a\}.

(18)

The Lagrangian on the simplex with multiplier
\mu
has
first-order conditions
q_{b}=p_{b}/\mu
and
q_{r}=q_{a}=(p_{r}+p_{a})/(2\mu)
;
the normalisation
\sum_{b}q_{b}=1/\mu=1
forces
\mu=1
,
yielding (
18
), and the positive-definite Hessian

\mathrm{diag}(p/q^{2})
ensures uniqueness. The KL value of this
projection is

\Phi(p_{a}):=p_{r}\log\frac{2p_{r}}{p_{r}+p_{a}}+p_{a}\log\frac{2p_{a}}{p_{r}+p_{a}}.

(19)

Step 2 (the optimal competitor is
a^{\star}
).

Differentiating (
19
) gives

\Phi^{\prime}(p_{a})=\log\frac{2p_{a}}{p_{r}+p_{a}}\leq 0
on
(0,p_{r})
, so

\Phi
is non-increasing in
p_{a}
and is minimised over
\{p_{a}:a\neq r\}

at
p_{a}=p_{(2)}=p_{a^{\star}}
. After midpointing on
a^{\star}
, the
remaining masses satisfy

q_{b}=p_{b}\leq p_{(2)}<(p_{r}+p_{(2)})/2=q_{r}=q_{a^{\star}}
for

b\notin\{r,a^{\star}\}
, confirming that the candidate lies in

H_{0,r}
. Hence midpointing
r
with the chosen
a^{\star}
yields a
global forward I-projection
P^{\star}
with

D(P\|P^{\star})=\Phi(p_{(2)})
(in case of ties at
p_{(2)}
each
choice of strongest competitor gives a distinct minimiser with the
same KL value), proving the first two displays.

Step 3 (small-gap expansion).

Write
q^{\star}:=(p_{r}+p_{(2)})/2=p_{r}-\delta/2
and

x:=\delta/(2q^{\star})\in(0,1)
, so
p_{r}/q^{\star}=1+x
and

p_{(2)}/q^{\star}=1-x
. The closed form factorises as

\Phi(p_{(2)})=q^{\star}f(x)
, where

f(x):=(1+x)\log(1+x)+(1-x)\log(1-x).

f
is even with
f^{\prime\prime}(x)=2/(1-x^{2})=2+2x^{2}+O(x^{4})
, hence

f(x)=x^{2}+x^{4}/6+O(x^{6})
about
0
. Substituting,

D(P\|P^{\star})=q^{\star}x^{2}+O(q^{\star}x^{4})=\frac{\delta^{2}}{4q^{\star}}+O\left(\frac{\delta^{4}}{q^{\star 3}}\right),

and
q^{\star}=p_{r}(1-\delta/(2p_{r}))=p_{r}(1+o(1))
as
\delta/p_{r}\to 0

yields the third display.
∎

Lemma C.10

(Stopped KL identity for the I-projection)
.

Under the assumptions of Lemma
C.9
, if
\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau]<\infty
,
then

D(P^{\tau}\|(P^{\star})^{\tau})=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau]D(P\|P^{\star}).

Proof.

P
and
P^{\star}
are mutually absolutely continuous on
\mathcal{A}

(since
q^{\star}\in(0,1)
when
p_{r}\in(0,1)
, and other masses
agree), and the log-likelihood

\ell(x):=\log(P(x)/P^{\star}(x))
is bounded. The stopping-event
factor cancels in the likelihood ratio, so Fubini and independence
give
D(P^{\tau}\|(P^{\star})^{\tau})=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau]\mathbb{E}_{P}[\ell(X_{1})]=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau]D(P\|P^{\star})
,
exactly as in Lemma
C.7
.
∎

Corollary C.11

(Sharp information-theoretic lower bound)
.

Under the hypotheses of Corollary
C.8
,

\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau]\geq\frac{\log(1/\varepsilon)}{D(P\|P^{\star})}=\frac{4p_{r}\log(1/\varepsilon)}{\delta^{2}}\bigl(1+o(1)\bigr)\qquad(\delta/p_{r}\to 0).

Proof.

Apply the same data-processing argument as in
Corollary
C.8
with
Q=P^{\star}
in place of
the swap distribution. Since
P^{\star}\in H_{0,r}

(Lemma
C.9
), Type-I validity gives

P^{\star}(\tau<\infty)\leq\varepsilon
, and the data-processing
inequality combined with Lemma
C.10
yields

\log(1/\varepsilon)\leq\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau]D(P\|P^{\star})
.
The asymptotic constant follows from Lemma
C.9
.
∎

C.4.
Information-Theoretic Lower Bound for the Unseen-Category Component

Lemma C.12

(Stopped KL identity for a hidden-competitor alternative)
.

Fix a distribution
P
on a countable category set
\mathcal{A}
with
p_{r}\in(0,1)
,
and let
h\in\mathcal{A}
satisfy
h\neq r
and
p_{h}=0
.
Define a second distribution
Q
by

q_{r}=\frac{p_{r}}{2},\qquad q_{h}=\frac{p_{r}}{2},\qquad q_{a}=p_{a}\quad\text{for }a\notin\{r,h\}.

Let
P^{\tau}
and
Q^{\tau}
denote the laws of the stopped experiment

(\tau,X_{1},\dots,X_{\tau})
under
P
and
Q
.
If
\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau]<\infty
, then

D(P^{\tau}\|Q^{\tau})=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau]p_{r}\log 2.

Proof.

By the same Wald argument as Lemma
C.7
,

D(P^{\tau}\|Q^{\tau})=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{P}[\ell(X_{1})]
with

\ell(x):=\log(P(x)/Q(x))
, where we set
\ell(h):=0
(an arbitrary
finite value on the
P
-null event
\{X=h\}
that does not affect any

P
-expectation). By construction
\ell(r)=\log 2
and

\ell(x)=0
for
x\neq r
, so

\mathbb{E}_{P}[\ell(X_{1})]=p_{r}\log 2
.
∎

Corollary C.13

(Information-theoretic lower bound for the unseen-category component)
.

Fix
p\in(0,1)
and
\delta\in(0,p)
, and assume the category set

\mathcal{A}
contains at least
\lceil(1-p)/(p-\delta)\rceil+2
distinct
non-target categories (so that an unused “hidden” label is
available; this holds automatically on countably infinite category sets).
Define

\mathcal{P}(p,\delta):=\left\{P:p_{r}=p,p_{(2)}\leq p-\delta\right\}.

Let
\tau
be any stopping time corresponding to a sequential test such that

\sup_{Q\in H_{0,r}}\mathbb{P}_{Q}(\tau<\infty)\leq\varepsilon,

and assume that, for every
P\in\mathcal{P}(p,\delta)
,

\mathbb{P}_{P}(\tau<\infty)=1\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau]<\infty.

Then

\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}(p,\delta)}\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau]\geq\frac{\log(1/\varepsilon)}{p\log 2}.

Proof.

Pick
m\geq(1-p)/(p-\delta)
distinct categories
b_{1},\dots,b_{m},h\in\mathcal{A}\setminus\{r\}
and set
P(r)=p
,
P(h)=0
,

P(b_{j})=(1-p)/m
, so
P\in\mathcal{P}(p,\delta)
.
The hidden-competitor distribution

Q(r)=Q(h)=p/2
,
Q(b_{j})=(1-p)/m
lies in
H_{0,r}
, so

Q(\tau<\infty)\leq\varepsilon
. Data processing combined with
Lemma
C.12
then gives

\log(1/\varepsilon)\leq D(P^{\tau}\|Q^{\tau})=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau]p\log 2
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.4
.

Fix
p\in(0,1/2]
,
\delta\in(0,p)
, and a level-
\varepsilon

sequential certifier
\tau^{\prime}
satisfying the assumptions of the
corollary. For notational simplicity, write
\tau:=\tau^{\prime}
within this
proof, and let

\mathcal{P}(p,\delta):=\left\{P:p_{r}=p,\quad p_{(2)}\leq p-\delta\right\}.

We combine two distinct least favorable subfamilies of
\mathcal{P}(p,\delta)
.

Step 1: Discrimination-hard instance.

Because
p\leq 1/2
, the quantity
1-2p+\delta
is nonnegative.
Choose an integer

m\geq\frac{1-2p+\delta}{p-\delta},

and distinct categories

a^{\star},b_{1},\dots,b_{m}\in\mathcal{A}\setminus\{r\}.

Define
P_{\mathrm{pw}}
by

P_{\mathrm{pw}}(r)=p,\qquad P_{\mathrm{pw}}(a^{\star})=p-\delta,\qquad P_{\mathrm{pw}}(b_{j})=\frac{1-2p+\delta}{m}\quad(j=1,\dots,m),

and
P_{\mathrm{pw}}(a)=0
otherwise.
Then
P_{\mathrm{pw}}\in\mathcal{P}(p,\delta)
and its runner-up satisfies

p_{(2)}=p-\delta>0
.
Corollary
C.8
therefore gives

\mathbb{E}_{P_{\mathrm{pw}}}[\tau]\geq\frac{\log(1/\varepsilon)}{\delta\log\bigl(p/(p-\delta)\bigr)}.

If
\delta\leq p/2
, then

\log\frac{p}{p-\delta}=-\log\left(1-\frac{\delta}{p}\right)\leq\frac{\delta/p}{1-\delta/p}\leq\frac{2\delta}{p},

whence

\mathbb{E}_{P_{\mathrm{pw}}}[\tau]\geq\frac{p\log(1/\varepsilon)}{2\delta^{2}}.

Step 2: Exploration-hard instance.

Corollary
C.13
provides another distribution

P_{\mathrm{un}}\in\mathcal{P}(p,\delta)
such that

\mathbb{E}_{P_{\mathrm{un}}}[\tau]\geq\frac{\log(1/\varepsilon)}{p\log 2}.

Step 3: Combine the two bounds.

If
\delta\leq p/2
, then
\max\{x,y\}\geq(x+y)/2
applied to the
two lower bounds gives the result directly.
If
\delta>p/2
, then
p/\delta^{2}\leq 4/p
, so

p/\delta^{2}+1/p\leq 5/p
, and
Corollary
C.13
alone gives

\sup_{P}\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau]\geq\log(1/\varepsilon)/(p\log 2)\geq(5\log 2)^{-1}\log(1/\varepsilon)(p/\delta^{2}+1/p)
.
Since
(5\log 2)^{-1}>1/4
, the same constant works in both cases.
∎

Appendix D
Detailed Power Analysis for the W-CITE

This appendix contains the proofs for the confidence-weighted extension
in Section
5.2
. We keep the notation parallel to
the unweighted analysis, replacing
(p_{r},p_{(2)},\delta)
by

(\mu_{r},\mu_{\star},\Delta_{w})
where appropriate.

Lemma D.1

(Expected maximum weighted competitor penalty)
.

Fix
\lambda\in(0,1)
and define

g_{\lambda}(w):=-\log(1-\lambda w)
for
w\in[0,1]
.
For each competitor
a\neq r
, let

H_{t}^{(\lambda)}(a):=\sum_{i=1}^{t}g_{\lambda}(W_{i})\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=a\}
,

v_{\lambda}(a):=\mathbb{E}[g_{\lambda}(W)\mathbf{1}\{X=a\}]
, and set

R_{t}^{(\lambda)}:=\max_{a\neq r}H_{t}^{(\lambda)}(a)
,

v_{\lambda}^{\star}:=\sup_{a\neq r}v_{\lambda}(a)
.
Then, for every integer
t\geq 1
,

\mathbb{E}[R_{t}^{(\lambda)}]\leq tv_{\lambda}^{\star}+2\sqrt{2}\bigl(-\log(1-\lambda)\bigr)\sqrt{t\log(t+1)}.

Lemma D.2

(Weighted LCB inversion at a fixed time)
.

Fix an integer
t\geq 1
and a number
q\in(0,1]
.

(a)
If
\widetilde{M}_{t}(q)>1
, then
\widehat{\mu}_{t}(r)>q
.

(b)
If
\widetilde{M}_{t}(q)\geq 1/\alpha_{r}
, then
\widetilde{L}_{t}(r)\geq q
.

D.1.
Weighted pairwise NSM property (Section
5
, item (i))

Proof.

Fix
a\neq r
and
\lambda\in(0,1)
. Since
W_{i}\in[0,1]
, each factor

1+\lambda W_{i}\bigl(\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=r\}-\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=a\}\bigr)

is nonnegative. Under
\mu_{r}\leq\mu_{a}
,

\mathbb{E}\left[1+\lambda W_{i}\bigl(\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=r\}-\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=a\}\bigr)\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]=1+\lambda(\mu_{r}-\mu_{a})\leq 1,

using independence of
(X_{i},W_{i})
from
\mathcal{F}_{i-1}
. Therefore

(\widetilde{E}_{t}^{(r,a)}(\lambda))_{t\geq 0}
is an NSM.
∎

D.2.
Weighted mixture NSM (Section
5
, item (i))

Proof.

For each fixed
a\neq r
and each

\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{pw}}
,

(\widetilde{E}_{t}^{(r,a)}(\lambda))_{t\geq 0}
is an NSM under

\mu_{r}\leq\mu_{a}
by the preceding proof. Since the weights

(w_{\lambda})
are nonnegative and sum to at most one,

\widetilde{E}_{t}^{(r,a)}=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{pw}}}w_{\lambda}\widetilde{E}_{t}^{(r,a)}(\lambda)

is also an NSM with initial value at most one. Ville’s inequality gives

\mathbb{P}_{P}\left(\sup_{t\geq 0}\widetilde{E}_{t}^{(r,a)}\geq\alpha^{-1}\right)\leq\alpha.

∎

D.3.
Weighted LCB monotonicity (Section
5
, item (ii))

Proof.

The proof is identical to the monotonicity part of
Lemma
C.3
:

\Lambda_{r}(q_{2})\subseteq\Lambda_{r}(q_{1})
and each factor

1+\lambda(W_{i}\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=r\}-q)
is pointwise larger when
q
is
smaller, so
\widetilde{M}_{t}(q_{1})\geq\widetilde{M}_{t}(q_{2})
.
∎

D.4.
Weighted LCB validity (Section
5
, item (ii))

Proof.

If
\mu_{r}=0
, then
W_{i}\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=r\}=0
almost surely for all
i
, so

\widehat{\mu}_{t}(r)=0
and
\widetilde{L}_{t}(r)=0
almost surely. Hence

\{\exists t:\widetilde{L}_{t}(r)>\mu_{r}\}
is empty. Assume

\mu_{r}>0
in the remainder.

The proof parallels that of Proposition
3.4

(Appendix
B.4
), with the following
substitutions:
\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=r\}
becomes
W_{i}\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=r\}
,

p_{r}
becomes
\mu_{r}
,
\widehat{p}_{t}(r)
becomes
\widehat{\mu}_{t}(r)
,
and the mixture weights
(w_{\lambda})
become
(v_{\lambda})
.
At the true value
q=\mu_{r}
, one has

\mathbb{E}[W_{i}\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=r\}-\mu_{r}]=0
, so each

\widetilde{M}_{t}(\mu_{r},\lambda)
is a nonnegative martingale with unit initial
value, and Ville’s inequality gives

\mathbb{P}(\sup_{t}\widetilde{M}_{t}(\mu_{r})\geq 1/\alpha_{r})\leq\alpha_{r}
.
The monotonicity from
the weighted LCB monotonicity property then implies

\{\exists t:\widetilde{L}_{t}(r)>\mu_{r}\}\subseteq\{\sup_{t}\widetilde{M}_{t}(\mu_{r})\geq 1/\alpha_{r}\}
.
∎

D.5.
Weighted unseen bound validity (Section
5
, item (iii))

Proof.

Since
\mu_{a}\leq p_{a}
for every
a\in\mathcal{A}
,

\Bigl\{\exists a,\exists t:N_{t}(a)=0\text{ and }\mu_{a}>U_{t}\Bigr\}\subseteq\Bigl\{\exists a,\exists t:N_{t}(a)=0\text{ and }p_{a}>U_{t}\Bigr\}.

The probability of the latter event is at most
\alpha_{u}
by
Proposition
3.6
.
∎

D.6.
Proof of Theorem
5.2

Proof.

The proof parallels that of Theorem
4.1

(Appendix
B.6
), with
p_{a}
replaced by
\mu_{a}

throughout and Lemma
B.3
replaced by the
all-competitors check (runner-up monotonicity fails for weighted
observations).

Fix
P\in H_{0,r}^{(w)}
. If
\mathcal{A}\setminus\{r\}=\emptyset
, then the
weighted null is vacuous. Otherwise, since

\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\mu_{a}=\mathbb{E}[W]\leq 1,

Lemma
B.1
implies that any positive value of

\mu_{\star}=\sup_{a\neq r}\mu_{a}
is attained. If
\mu_{\star}>0
, choose

a^{\star}\neq r
with
\mu_{a^{\star}}=\mu_{\star}\geq\mu_{r}
. If

\mu_{\star}=0
, then
P\in H_{0,r}^{(w)}
implies
\mu_{r}=0
, and any

a^{\star}\neq r
is a valid witness with

\mu_{a^{\star}}\geq\mu_{r}
.
Decompose

\{\tau^{(w)}<\infty\}\subseteq\underbrace{\{\tau^{(w)}<\infty,\ a^{\star}\in\mathcal{A}_{\tau^{(w)}}\}}_{E_{1}}\cup\underbrace{\{\tau^{(w)}<\infty,\ a^{\star}\notin\mathcal{A}_{\tau^{(w)}}\}}_{E_{2}}.

Step 1 (bounding
\mathbb{P}(E_{1})
).

On
E_{1}
, the weighted pairwise stopping condition at
\tau^{(w)}

applied to
every
observed competitor gives

\widetilde{E}_{\tau^{(w)}}^{(r,a^{\star})}\geq 1/\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}
.
The process
(\widetilde{E}_{t}^{(r,a^{\star})})_{t\geq 0}
is an NSM under

\mu_{r}\leq\mu_{a^{\star}}
by the weighted mixture NSM property
(Section
5
, item (i)), with initial value
\leq 1
.
Ville’s inequality (
11
) yields
\mathbb{P}(E_{1})\leq\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}
.

Step 2 (bounding
\mathbb{P}(E_{2})
).

Define

F_{r}:=\{\exists t\geq 1:\widetilde{L}_{t}(r)>\mu_{r}\},\quad F_{u}:=\{\exists a\in\mathcal{A},\ \exists t\geq 1:N_{t}(a)=0,\ \mu_{a}>U_{t}\}.

Weighted LCB validity gives
\mathbb{P}(F_{r})\leq\alpha_{r}
, and weighted
unseen-bound validity gives
\mathbb{P}(F_{u})\leq\alpha_{u}
.
On
E_{2}\setminus(F_{r}\cup F_{u})
, the LCB–unseen part of the weighted
stopping rule gives

\widetilde{L}_{\tau^{(w)}}(r)>U_{\tau^{(w)}}
; combined with

F_{r}^{c}
and
F_{u}^{c}
(recall
a^{\star}
is unseen at
\tau^{(w)}
),

\mu_{a^{\star}}\leq U_{\tau^{(w)}}<\widetilde{L}_{\tau^{(w)}}(r)\leq\mu_{r},

contradicting
\mu_{a^{\star}}\geq\mu_{r}
. Hence

E_{2}\subseteq F_{r}\cup F_{u}
and
\mathbb{P}(E_{2})\leq\alpha_{r}+\alpha_{u}
.

Step 3 (union bound).

\mathbb{P}(\tau^{(w)}<\infty)\leq\mathbb{P}(E_{1})+\mathbb{P}(E_{2})\leq\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}+\alpha_{r}+\alpha_{u}\leq\varepsilon
.
∎

D.7.
Proof of Theorem
5.4
: almost-sure stopping part

Proof.

Lemma
D.3
(a) gives

\gamma_{\mathrm{pw}}:=\inf_{a\neq r}\mathbb{E}[\log(1+\lambda_{\mathrm{pw}}W(\mathbf{1}\{X=r\}-\mathbf{1}\{X=a\}))]\geq\lambda_{\mathrm{pw}}\Delta_{w}/2>0
,
and Lemma
D.3
(c) with
q_{0}=\mu_{r}/2

gives

\nu_{r}:=\mathbb{E}[\log(1+\lambda_{r}(W\mathbf{1}\{X=r\}-q_{0}))]\geq\lambda_{r}\mu_{r}/4>0
.
We now show that these positive drifts force
\mathbb{P}(\tau^{(w)}<\infty)=1
.

Fix
\lambda:=\lambda_{\mathrm{pw}}
, and let

w_{\mathrm{pw}}
denote its mixture weight in
Section
5
, item (i).

Step 1: Pairwise condition.

For each
i
, set

A_{i}:=\log(1+\lambda W_{i})\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=r\}
and

B_{i}(a):=-\log(1-\lambda W_{i})\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=a\}
, so that

\log\widetilde{E}_{t}^{(r,a)}(\lambda)=\textstyle\sum_{i=1}^{t}A_{i}-\sum_{i=1}^{t}B_{i}(a).

(20)

Write
u:=\mathbb{E}[A_{1}]
,
v_{a}:=\mathbb{E}[B_{1}(a)]
, and

v^{\star}:=\sup_{a\neq r}v_{a}
.
The definition of
\gamma_{\mathrm{pw}}
gives

u-v^{\star}\geq\gamma_{\mathrm{pw}}>0
.

We control
\max_{a\neq r}t^{-1}\sum_{i}B_{i}(a)
on the countable
category set by a tail-truncation argument.
Since
\sum_{a\neq r}v_{a}\leq-\log(1-\lambda)<\infty
,
for each
m\geq 1
there is a finite
F_{m}\subset\mathcal{A}\setminus\{r\}

with
\sum_{a\notin F_{m}}v_{a}<1/m
.
Define the tail process

R_{t}^{(m)}:=\sum_{i}-\log(1-\lambda W_{i})\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}\notin F_{m}\cup\{r\}\}
.
By the Strong Law of Large Numbers,
t^{-1}\sum_{i}A_{i}\to u
,

t^{-1}\sum_{i}B_{i}(a)\to v_{a}
for each fixed
a
,
and
t^{-1}R_{t}^{(m)}\to\sum_{a\notin F_{m}}v_{a}<1/m
,
all almost surely.
On the probability-one intersection of these countably many events,

\limsup_{t}\max_{a\neq r}t^{-1}\sum_{i}B_{i}(a)\leq\max\{v^{\star},1/m\}
for every
m
;
letting
m\to\infty
gives the limsup
\leq v^{\star}
.
Combining with (
20
),

\liminf_{t}\min_{a\neq r}t^{-1}\log\widetilde{E}_{t}^{(r,a)}(\lambda)\geq u-v^{\star}\geq\gamma_{\mathrm{pw}}>0.

Since
\mathcal{A}_{t}\setminus\{r\}\subseteq\mathcal{A}\setminus\{r\}
and

\widetilde{E}_{t}^{(r,a)}\geq w_{\mathrm{pw}}\widetilde{E}_{t}^{(r,a)}(\lambda)
,
the positive liminf of the normalized log e-process implies
there exists a.s. a finite
T_{\mathrm{pw}}^{\prime}
after which

\min_{a\in\mathcal{A}_{t}\setminus\{r\}}\widetilde{E}_{t}^{(r,a)}\geq 1/\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}
.

Step 2: Weighted LCB condition.

Fix
\lambda:=\lambda_{r}
with mixture weight
v_{\lambda_{r}}>0
and
set
C_{i}:=\log(1+\lambda(W_{i}\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=r\}-q_{0}))
;
each
C_{i}
is bounded since
\lambda<1/q_{0}
(Lemma
D.3
(c))
and
W_{i}\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=r\}\in[0,1]
.
Part (c) of Lemma
D.3
gives

\mathbb{E}[C_{i}]=\nu_{r}\geq\lambda\mu_{r}/4>0
, so by the SLLN

t^{-1}\log\widetilde{M}_{t}(q_{0},\lambda)=t^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{t}C_{i}\to\nu_{r}

a.s., hence
\widetilde{M}_{t}(q_{0},\lambda)\to\infty
and

\widetilde{M}_{t}(q_{0})\geq v_{\lambda_{r}}\widetilde{M}_{t}(q_{0},\lambda)\geq 1/\alpha_{r}

eventually a.s. An independent SLLN on
W_{i}\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=r\}
gives

\widehat{\mu}_{t}(r)\to\mu_{r}>q_{0}
, so
\widehat{\mu}_{t}(r)\geq q_{0}

eventually, and the weighted LCB construction then yields

\widetilde{L}_{t}(r)\geq q_{0}
for all large
t
almost surely. Let
T^{\prime}_{r}
denote
the resulting (random, a.s. finite) eventual time after which both

\widetilde{M}_{t}(q_{0})\geq 1/\alpha_{r}
and
\widetilde{L}_{t}(r)\geq q_{0}
hold.

Step 3: Unseen upper bound.

The sequence
(U_{t})_{t\geq 1}
is deterministic and

U_{t}\downarrow 0
as
t\to\infty
. Since
q_{0}>0
, there exists a
deterministic index
T_{u}
such that

U_{t}<q_{0}\qquad\forall t\geq T_{u}.

Step 4: Combine.

Choose
t
large enough that

t\geq T_{\mathrm{pw}}^{\prime}\vee T^{\prime}_{r}\vee T_{u}
.
Then the pairwise threshold is met,

\widetilde{L}_{t}(r)\geq q_{0}>U_{t}
,
and both stopping conditions hold, giving

\mathbb{P}(\tau^{(w)}<\infty)=1
.
∎

Lemma D.3

(Weighted small-bet drift bounds)
.

Assume
\Delta_{w}>0
.

(a)

Pairwise:

if
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{pw}}
satisfies

\lambda\leq\Delta_{w}/\bigl(2(\mu_{r}+\mu_{\star})\bigr)
, then

\inf_{a\neq r}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\log\bigl(1+\lambda W(\mathbf{1}\{X=r\}-\mathbf{1}\{X=a\})\bigr)\bigr]\geq\lambda\Delta_{w}/2>0.

(b)

LCB:

for any
q_{0}\in(0,\mu_{r})
and any
\lambda\in\Lambda_{r}
with

\lambda\leq(\mu_{r}-q_{0})/(2\mu_{r})
, one has
\lambda<1/q_{0}
and

\mathbb{E}\bigl[\log\bigl(1+\lambda(W\mathbf{1}\{X=r\}-q_{0})\bigr)\bigr]\geq\lambda(\mu_{r}-q_{0})/2>0.

(c)

Universal LCB grid point:

with
q_{0}:=\mu_{r}/2
in part (b), the condition reduces to

\lambda_{r}\in(0,1/4]
, and

\mathbb{E}\bigl[\log\bigl(1+\lambda_{r}(W\mathbf{1}\{X=r\}-\mu_{r}/2)\bigr)\bigr]\geq\lambda_{r}\mu_{r}/4>0.

Proof.

All three parts follow from the quadratic lower bound

\log(1+\xi)\geq\xi-\xi^{2}
on
[-1/2,1/2]

(Lemma
C.4
), exactly as in
Proposition
C.5
, with two additional
ingredients:
W\in[0,1]
implies
W^{2}\leq W
, and the squared
indicators satisfy
