(\mathbf{1}\{X=r\}-\mathbf{1}\{X=a\})^{2}=\mathbf{1}\{X=r\}+\mathbf{1}\{X=a\}
.

(a)

Set
\xi_{a}:=\lambda W(\mathbf{1}\{X=r\}-\mathbf{1}\{X=a\})
, so

|\xi_{a}|\leq\lambda\leq 1/2
. Taking expectations of

\log(1+\xi_{a})\geq\xi_{a}-\xi_{a}^{2}
and using
W^{2}\leq W
,

\mathbb{E}[\log(1+\xi_{a})]\geq\lambda(\mu_{r}-\mu_{a})-\lambda^{2}(\mu_{r}+\mu_{a})\geq\lambda\Delta_{w}-\lambda^{2}(\mu_{r}+\mu_{\star}).

The hypothesis gives

\lambda^{2}(\mu_{r}+\mu_{\star})\leq\lambda\Delta_{w}/2
, hence

\mathbb{E}[\log(1+\xi_{a})]\geq\lambda\Delta_{w}/2
uniformly in
a
.

(b)

Since
\mu_{r}\leq 1
,
(\mu_{r}-q_{0})/(2\mu_{r})<1/2<1/q_{0}
, so the
hypothesis implies
\lambda<1/2
and
\lambda<1/q_{0}
.
Setting
Y:=W\mathbf{1}\{X=r\}
and
\xi:=\lambda(Y-q_{0})
, the same
quadratic bound and
Y^{2}\leq Y
yield

\mathbb{E}[\log(1+\xi)]\geq\lambda(\mu_{r}-q_{0})-\lambda^{2}\mu_{r}\geq\lambda(\mu_{r}-q_{0})/2.

(c)

Specializing (b) to
q_{0}=\mu_{r}/2
gives

(\mu_{r}-q_{0})/(2\mu_{r})=1/4
, so
\lambda_{r}\leq 1/4
verifies the
hypothesis and the conclusion follows.
∎

D.8.
Proof of Lemma
D.1

Proof.

The proof follows the same symmetrization argument as
Lemma
C.2
, applied to the bounded function class

\{f_{a}(x,w):=g_{\lambda}(w)\mathbf{1}\{x=a\}:a\neq r\}

in place of
\{\mathbf{1}\{x=a\}\}
. The key modifications are:

•

The centering term
tp_{(2)}
becomes
tv_{\lambda}^{\star}
,
where
v_{\lambda}^{\star}:=\sup_{a\neq r}\mathbb{E}[g_{\lambda}(W)\mathbf{1}\{X=a\}]
.

•

The per-coordinate bound
\|v_{a}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
becomes

\|v_{a}\|_{\infty}\leq g_{\lambda}(1)=-\log(1-\lambda)
,
so the sub-Gaussian parameter scales by
-\log(1-\lambda)
.

•

The Rademacher complexity bound remains

O(\sqrt{t\log(t+1)})
because there are still at most
t+1

distinct vectors in the function class evaluated on
t
samples.

Optimizing the Donsker–Varadhan parameter
s
as in
Lemma
C.2
yields the stated bound.
∎

D.9.
Proof of Lemma
D.2

Proof.

(a)

Assume
\widehat{\mu}_{t}(r)\leq q
.
Set
Y_{i}:=W_{i}\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=r\}
.
By Jensen’s inequality (concavity of
\log
),

t^{-1}\log\widetilde{M}_{t}(q,\lambda)\leq\log(1+\lambda(\widehat{\mu}_{t}(r)-q))\leq 0

for every
\lambda\in\Lambda_{r}(q)
.
Summing gives
\widetilde{M}_{t}(q)\leq\sum v_{\lambda}\leq 1
,
contradicting
\widetilde{M}_{t}(q)>1
.

(b)

If
\widetilde{M}_{t}(q)\geq 1/\alpha_{r}>1
, part (a) gives

\widehat{\mu}_{t}(r)>q
, so
q\in(0,\widehat{\mu}_{t}(r)]
and

q
belongs to the set defining
\widetilde{L}_{t}(r)
; hence
\widetilde{L}_{t}(r)\geq q
.
∎

D.10.
Proof of Theorem
5.4
: expected stopping-time bound

Proof.

We use the same structure as the proof of
Theorem
4.3
, with

(p_{r},p_{(2)},\delta)
replaced by

(\mu_{r},\mu_{\star},\Delta_{w})
.

Step 1: pairwise condition.

Let

\lambda:=\lambda_{\mathrm{pw}}\in[\Delta_{w}/8,\Delta_{w}/4]
and let

w_{\lambda}
be its mixture weight. Set

B_{\mathrm{pw}}^{(w)}:=\log\{1/(\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}w_{\lambda})\}.

For each competitor
a\neq r
, write

A_{i}:=\log(1+\lambda W_{i})\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=r\},\qquad B_{i}(a):=-\log(1-\lambda W_{i})\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=a\}.

The relevant fixed-
\lambda
log pairwise statistic is

S_{t}^{(w)}:=\sum_{i=1}^{t}A_{i}-\max_{a\neq r}\sum_{i=1}^{t}B_{i}(a).

If
S_{t}^{(w)}\geq B_{\mathrm{pw}}^{(w)}
, then the weighted
all-competitors pairwise condition holds at time
t
.

Lemma
D.3
gives the drift lower bound

\inf_{a\neq r}\mathbb{E}\log\{1+\lambda W(\mathbf{1}\{X=r\}-\mathbf{1}\{X=a\})\}\geq\lambda\Delta_{w}/2\geq c\Delta_{w}^{2}.

Combining this with Lemma
D.1
yields

\mathbb{E}[S_{t}^{(w)}]\geq c_{0}\Delta_{w}^{2}t-C_{0}\Delta_{w}\sqrt{t\log(t+1)}.

Changing one observation changes
S_{t}^{(w)}
by at most

C_{1}\Delta_{w}
, so McDiarmid’s inequality and
Lemma
C.1
imply that, for a universal constant
C
,

\mathbb{P}\{\text{weighted pairwise condition fails at time }t\}\leq(et)^{-2}

whenever

t\geq T_{\mathrm{pw}}^{(w)}:=C\frac{\log\{1/(\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}w_{\lambda})\}+\log(1/\Delta_{w})}{\Delta_{w}^{2}}.

Step 2: LCB–unseen condition.

Set
q_{0}:=\mu_{r}/2
. The grid condition gives

\lambda_{r}\in[1/32,1/16]\subset\Lambda_{r}(q_{0})
with weight

v_{\lambda_{r}}
. Let

Y_{i}:=W_{i}\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=r\},\qquad A_{t}:=\left\{\frac{3}{4}\mu_{r}t\leq\sum_{i=1}^{t}Y_{i}\leq\frac{5}{4}\mu_{r}t\right\}.

Since
Y_{i}\in[0,1]
and
\mathbb{E}Y_{i}=\mu_{r}
, Bernstein’s inequality gives

\mathbb{P}(A_{t}^{c})\leq 2\exp(-c\mu_{r}t).

On
A_{t}
, the single weighted LCB factor satisfies

\log\widetilde{M}_{t}(q_{0},\lambda_{r})=\sum_{i=1}^{t}\log\{1+\lambda_{r}(Y_{i}-q_{0})\}\geq c^{\prime}\mu_{r}t

for a universal constant
c^{\prime}>0
. Thus

\widetilde{M}_{t}(q_{0})\geq v_{\lambda_{r}}\widetilde{M}_{t}(q_{0},\lambda_{r})\geq\alpha_{r}^{-1}

once

t\geq C\mu_{r}^{-1}\log\{1/(\alpha_{r}v_{\lambda_{r}})\}.

Moreover, the unchanged unseen bound satisfies
U_{t}\leq\mu_{r}/4<q_{0}

once

t\geq C\mu_{r}^{-1}\log\{1/(\alpha_{u}\mu_{r})\}.

Combining these bounds with Lemma
C.1
, we obtain

\mathbb{P}\{\widetilde{L}_{t}(r)\leq U_{t}\}\leq(et)^{-2}

for all

t\geq T_{\mathrm{rare}}^{(w)}:=C\frac{\log(1/\alpha_{r})+\log(1/\alpha_{u})+\log(1/v_{\lambda_{r}})+\log(1/\mu_{r})}{\mu_{r}}.

Step 3: combine.

Let

T_{\mathrm{cl}}^{(w)}:=\max\{T_{\mathrm{pw}}^{(w)},T_{\mathrm{rare}}^{(w)}\}.

For all
t\geq T_{\mathrm{cl}}^{(w)}
,

\mathbb{P}(\tau^{(w)}>t)\leq 2(et)^{-2}.

The tail-sum identity gives

\mathbb{E}[\tau^{(w)}]\leq T_{\mathrm{cl}}^{(w)}+1.

Substituting

\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}=\alpha_{r}=\alpha_{u}=\varepsilon/3
and using the
selected-weight lower bounds yields

\mathbb{E}[\tau^{(w)}]=O\left(\frac{\log(1/\varepsilon)+\log(1/\Delta_{w})}{\Delta_{w}^{2}}+\frac{\log(1/\varepsilon)+\log(1/\mu_{r})}{\mu_{r}}\right).

∎

Corollary D.4

(Independent confidence scores)
.

Suppose
W
is independent of
X
and let
\bar{w}:=\mathbb{E}[W]>0
. If

r
is the unweighted unique mode with gap

\delta:=p_{r}-p_{(2)}>0
, then

\mu_{r}=\bar{w}p_{r},\qquad\mu_{\star}=\bar{w}p_{(2)},\qquad\Delta_{w}=\bar{w}\delta.

Thus Theorem
5.4
applies whenever the
weighted grid condition holds, equivalently when

\lambda_{\mathrm{pw}}\in\left[\frac{\bar{w}\delta}{8},\frac{\bar{w}\delta}{4}\right]\quad\text{and}\quad\lambda_{r}\in[1/32,1/16]

are available on the grids.

D.11.
Proof of Corollary
D.4

Proof.

Independence gives

\mu_{a}=\mathbb{E}[W\mathbf{1}\{X=a\}]=\bar{w}p_{a}\qquad\forall a\in\mathcal{A}.

Therefore

\mu_{r}=\bar{w}p_{r},\qquad\mu_{\star}=\bar{w}p_{(2)},\qquad\Delta_{w}=\bar{w}\delta.

Substituting these identities into the grid condition and the rate in
Theorem
5.4
proves the claim.
∎

Proposition D.5

(Recovery of the unweighted CITE)
.

Suppose
W_{i}\equiv 1
almost surely. Then

\mu_{a}=p_{a}
for every
a\in\mathcal{A}
;

\widetilde{E}_{t}^{(r,a)}(\lambda)=(1+\lambda)^{N_{t}(r)}(1-\lambda)^{N_{t}(a)}
;

\widehat{\mu}_{t}(r)=\widehat{p}_{t}(r)
;
and the weighted stopping rule agrees with the all-competitors
version of the unweighted CITE, with runner-up monotonicity reducing
the pairwise check to the empirical runner-up.

D.12.
Proof of Proposition
D.5

Proof.

Claim (i): Mass equality.

If
W_{i}\equiv 1
almost surely, then

\mu_{a}=\mathbb{E}[W\mathbf{1}\{X=a\}]=\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}\{X=a\}]=p_{a}\quad\forall a\in\mathcal{A}.

Claim (ii): Pairwise e-process recovery.

Under
W_{i}\equiv 1
, the weighted e-process simplifies:

\widetilde{Z}_{i}^{(r,a)}=W_{i}(\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=r\}-\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=a\})=\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=r\}-\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=a\}.

Thus

\widetilde{E}_{t}^{(r,a)}(\lambda)=\prod_{i=1}^{t}\bigl(1+\lambda\widetilde{Z}_{i}^{(r,a)}\bigr)=\prod_{i=1}^{t}\bigl(1+\lambda(\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=r\}-\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=a\})\bigr)=(1+\lambda)^{N_{t}(r)}(1-\lambda)^{N_{t}(a)}.

Claim (iii): Empirical mean equality.

\widehat{\mu}_{t}(r)=\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=1}^{t}W_{i}\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=r\}=\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=1}^{t}\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=r\}=\widehat{p}_{t}(r).

Substituting
W_{i}\equiv 1
into the weighted LCB e-process yields

\widetilde{M}_{t}(q,\lambda)=\prod_{i=1}^{t}(1+\lambda(\mathbf{1}\{X_{i}=r\}-q)),

which is identical to the unweighted LCB e-process after dropping the tilde.

Claim (iv): Stopping rule recovery.

From (ii), in the unweighted specialization the weighted pairwise condition

\widetilde{E}_{t}^{(r,a)}=\sum_{\lambda}w_{\lambda}\widetilde{E}_{t}^{(r,a)}(\lambda)\geq 1/\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}\quad\text{for all }a\in\mathcal{A}_{t}\setminus\{r\}

checks the same pairwise e-values as the unweighted all-competitors CITE.
By Lemma
B.3
, those e-values are monotone in the competitor count, so among observed competitors the minimum is
attained at the empirical runner-up. Thus the all-competitors pairwise check reduces
to a single empirical runner-up, recovering the original unweighted
fixed-target CITE.
∎

Appendix E
Comparison with Martingale Majority Certificates

E.1.
The MMC Hypothesis

The top-
m
MMC
(
Cordero-Encinar and Duncan 2025
)
simultaneously tests the leader
against
m-1
pairwise competitors and the aggregated residual mass.
The alternative certified upon stopping is

\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{MMC},m}:=\{P:p_{(1)}>p_{(i)}\forall i=2,\ldots,m\}\cap\{P:p_{(1)}>s_{m}\},\qquad s_{m}:=\sum_{j>m}p_{(j)}.

(21)

Proposition E.1

(Strict inclusion)
.

Suppose the target
r
coincides with the (unique) modal label

p_{r}=p_{(1)}
, equivalently
r
is the unique maximiser of

a\mapsto p_{a}
. Then for every
m\geq 2
,

\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{MMC},m}\subsetneq H_{1,r}
;
without the unique-mode-target identification, this strict inclusion
must be read with
H_{1,r}
replaced by

\bigcup_{r^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}H_{1,r^{\prime}}
.

Remark E.2

(The diffuse-tail regime)
.

Distributions in
H_{1,r}\setminus\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{MMC},m}
have a
unique mode that does not dominate the aggregate tail:

p_{(1)}>p_{(2)}
but
p_{(1)}\leq s_{m}
. Several of our LLM
self-consistency settings (Section
6
) fall into
this regime, with the mode-answer carrying
20
–
30\%
of the mass
and thousands of alternative answers sharing the remainder. For each fixed
m
, the MMC controls its certification rate at level
\alpha
on any such
P
: its residual-mass channel at that
m

tests the null
\{p_{(1)}\leq s_{m}\}
, which is true under
P
, so the
corresponding e-process cannot grow past the rejection threshold with
probability
>\alpha
. An MMC variant that tries multiple
m
in
parallel pays a Bonferroni-style cost over the active tuples
(Appendix
E.3
).

E.2.
The Switching-Null Challenge

At each round
n
, the MMC selects the empirical leader
A_{n-1}

and runner-up
B_{n-1}
(both
\mathcal{F}_{n-1}
-measurable), maintaining
e-processes:
e_{n}^{\mathrm{run}}
(leader vs. runner-up) and
e_{n}^{\mathrm{oth}}
(leader vs. residual mass).
The per-round nulls are:

R_{n}:=\{p_{A_{n-1}}\leq p_{B_{n-1}}\},\quad O_{n}:=\Bigl\{p_{A_{n-1}}\leq\sum\nolimits_{j\notin\{A_{n-1},B_{n-1}\}}p_{j}\Bigr\}.

(22)

Theorem 3.1 of
Cordero-Encinar and Duncan 2025
establishes that

(e_{n}^{\mathrm{run}})
is an NSM under the all-rounds null

H_{0}^{\mathrm{run}}:=\bigcap_{n\geq 1}R_{n}
, and similarly for the residual-mass channel.
Corollary 3.2 proves

\sup_{P\in H_{0}}\mathbb{P}_{P}(N<\infty)\leq\varepsilon,\qquad H_{0}:=H_{0}^{\mathrm{run}}\cup H_{0}^{\mathrm{oth}}.

(23)

The intersection-union structure (stop requires
both
channels, null is a
union
)
gives
\varepsilon
without Bonferroni.

The challenge lies in bridging from (
23
) to the
adaptive misclassification guarantee

\mathbb{P}(A_{\tau-1}\neq a^{\star})\leq\varepsilon
for the certified
pre-round leader (cf. Proposition
E.3
below).
At any fixed time
n
, misclassification of the pre-round leader
satisfies

\{A_{n-1}\neq a^{\star}\}\subseteq R_{n}\cup O_{n}.

(24)

However, Corollary 3.2 controls the all-rounds event

H_{0}=\Bigl(\bigcap_{t\geq 1}R_{t}\Bigr)\cup\Bigl(\bigcap_{t\geq 1}O_{t}\Bigr),

which is logically distinct from the existential per-round inclusion:

(\forall tR_{t})\lor(\forall tO_{t})\quad\not\Longleftarrow\quad\exists n(R_{n}\lor O_{n}).

(25)

Bridging this gap requires a
switching-null reduction
showing that
misclassification at
\tau
is contained in the all-rounds null.
Since the labels
(A_{n-1},B_{n-1})
are data-dependent and may switch while
e-processes accumulate evidence without restart, this reduction is nontrivial but feasible.
It introduces Bonferroni costs absent from CITE.

E.3.
Tuple-Indexed Construction

To handle the switching-null challenge,
index e-processes by tuple
: let

\mathcal{T}:=\bigl\{(m,a,b_{1},\ldots,b_{m-1}):m\geq 2,\ a\in\mathcal{A},\ b_{1},\ldots,b_{m-1}\in\mathcal{A}\setminus\{a\}\text{ pairwise distinct}\bigr\}

denote the (countable) set of admissible tuples and assign each

\ell=(m,a,b_{1},\ldots,b_{m-1})\in\mathcal{T}
its own e-process,
with multiplicative factor
1
on rounds where the current tuple differs from
\ell
.

Proposition E.3

(Tuple-indexed repair)
.

Assume the data-generating distribution has a unique mode

a^{\star}\in\arg\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}p_{a}
. Let

\{\alpha_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in\mathcal{T}}
satisfy

\sum_{\ell}\alpha_{\ell}\leq\varepsilon
.
For each
\ell
, maintain separate tuple-indexed e-processes

E_{t}^{\ell,i}
(pairwise, slot
i
) and

E_{t}^{\ell,\mathrm{oth}}
(residual mass),
active only when
L_{t-1}=\ell
.
Define

\tau:=\inf\Bigl\{n:\exists\ell\in\mathcal{T}\text{ s.t.\ }L_{n-1}=\ell,E_{n}^{\ell,i}\geq 1/\alpha_{\ell}\forall i,E_{n}^{\ell,\mathrm{oth}}\geq 1/\alpha_{\ell}\Bigr\}.

Let
A_{\tau-1}
denote the leader component of the active tuple

L_{\tau-1}
(the pre-round empirical leader at the certifying time).
Then
\mathbb{P}(\tau<\infty,A_{\tau-1}\neq a^{\star})\leq\varepsilon
.

The Bonferroni cost.

Each tuple receives error allocation
\alpha_{\ell}
, yielding threshold
1/\alpha_{\ell}
.
With
\pi_{\ell}:=\alpha_{\ell}/\varepsilon
:

\log\frac{1}{\alpha_{\ell}}=\underbrace{\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}_{\text{base}}+\underbrace{\log\frac{1}{\pi_{\ell}}}_{\text{multiplicity penalty}}.

(26)

Under uniform allocation over
K
tuples, the multiplicity penalty is
\log K
.
With
L_{t}
observed categories:
K_{m,t}=L_{t}\binom{L_{t}-1}{m-1}=O(L_{t}^{m})
,
yielding additional sample complexity of order
m\log L_{t}/I_{\ell}
,
where
I_{\ell}
denotes the per-observation KL information.

Contrast with CITE.

CITE avoids this multiplicity cost: with
r
fixed a priori, Ville’s inequality
applies to a single e-process with threshold
1/\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}

and no multiplicity correction.

E.4.
Structural Comparison Summary

Tuple-Indexed MMC

CITE (ours)

Hypothesis

p_{(1)}>p_{(i)}
for all
i\leq m
and
p_{(1)}>s_{m}

p_{(1)}>p_{(2)}
(exact unique mode)

Target selection

Adaptive (tuple may switch)

Fixed target
r
a priori

Multiplicity cost

\log K_{m,t}
threshold inflation

None

Diffuse-tail power

\leq\alpha
(
p_{(1)}\leq s_{m}

\Rightarrow
null true; Ville bound)

Full (pairwise margin only)

Unseen control

Aggregated into residual mass

Explicit:
L_{t}(r)>U_{t}

Table 1.
Structural comparison of the tuple-indexed MMC and CITE.

E.5.
Proof of Proposition
E.1

Proof.

Inclusion
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{MMC},m}\subseteq H_{1,r}
.

Any
P\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{MMC},m}
satisfies

p_{(1)}>p_{(i)}
for every
i\in\{2,\ldots,m\}
, and

p_{(1)}>s_{m}\geq p_{(j)}
for every
j>m
; combining,
p_{r}=p_{(1)}

strictly dominates every other category, i.e.
P\in H_{1,r}
.

Strict inclusion.
Fix
m\geq 2
and
K\geq 2m
, and consider
the perturbed-uniform distribution

p_{(1)}:=\frac{1}{K}+\eta,\qquad p_{(i)}:=\frac{1}{K}-\frac{\eta}{K-1}\quad(i\geq 2),\qquad\eta\in(0,\tfrac{1}{K}),

so that
\sum_{i}p_{(i)}=1
and
p_{(1)}>p_{(2)}
.
Then
P\in H_{1,r}
(since
r
is strictly the unique mode), while

s_{m}=\sum_{j>m}p_{(j)}=(K-m)\left(\tfrac{1}{K}-\tfrac{\eta}{K-1}\right)\geq\tfrac{K-m}{K}-\eta\geq\tfrac{1}{2}-\eta>\tfrac{1}{K}+\eta=p_{(1)}

for sufficiently small
\eta
(using
K\geq 2m
and
K\geq 2
).
Hence
P\notin\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{MMC},m}
, proving strict inclusion.
∎

E.6.
Proof of Proposition
E.3

Proof.

Fix a tuple
\ell=(a,b_{1},\ldots,b_{m-1})\in\mathcal{T}
with leader
a
and
slots
b_{1},\ldots,b_{m-1}
.
Define its stopping time and misclassification event

\tau_{\ell}:=\inf\bigl\{n\geq 1:L_{n-1}=\ell,\ E_{n}^{\ell,i}\geq 1/\alpha_{\ell}\ \forall i,\ E_{n}^{\ell,\mathrm{oth}}\geq 1/\alpha_{\ell}\bigr\},\quad G_{\ell}:=\{\tau=\tau_{\ell}<\infty,\ a\neq a^{\star}\}.

Suppose
G_{\ell}
occurs. Since
a\neq a^{\star}
and
a^{\star}
lies in

\mathcal{A}
, exactly one of the following holds:

Case (i):
a^{\star}=b_{i}
for some
i\in\{1,\ldots,m-1\}
.

Then the pairwise null for slot
i
is

R^{(\ell,i)}:=\{p_{a}\leq p_{b_{i}}\}
, which is
true
because

p_{a}<p_{a^{\star}}=p_{b_{i}}
(as
a^{\star}
is the unique mode). Under

R^{(\ell,i)}
the pairwise e-process
(E_{n}^{\ell,i})_{n\geq 0}
is an NSM
with initial value
\leq 1
, so Ville’s inequality gives

\mathbb{P}(E_{\tau_{\ell}}^{\ell,i}\geq 1/\alpha_{\ell})\leq\alpha_{\ell}
.
Hence
\mathbb{P}(G_{\ell}\cap\text{Case (i)})\leq\alpha_{\ell}
.

Case (ii):
a^{\star}\notin\{a,b_{1},\ldots,b_{m-1}\}
.

Then the residual-mass null

O^{(\ell)}:=\{p_{a}\leq\sum_{j\notin\{a,b_{1},\ldots,b_{m-1}\}}p_{j}\}

is true, since the sum on the right side includes
p_{a^{\star}}>p_{a}
.
Under
O^{(\ell)}
,
(E_{n}^{\ell,\mathrm{oth}})_{n\geq 0}
is an NSM with
initial value
\leq 1
, and Ville gives

\mathbb{P}(E_{\tau_{\ell}}^{\ell,\mathrm{oth}}\geq 1/\alpha_{\ell})\leq\alpha_{\ell}
.
Hence
\mathbb{P}(G_{\ell}\cap\text{Case (ii)})\leq\alpha_{\ell}
.

Combining both cases,
\mathbb{P}(G_{\ell})\leq\alpha_{\ell}
.

Mutual exclusion across tuples.

At the stopping time
\tau
, only the tuple

L_{\tau-1}
has
L_{\tau-1}=\ell
, so the events

\{\tau=\tau_{\ell},L_{\tau_{\ell}-1}=\ell\}
are disjoint across
\ell
.
Therefore

\mathbb{P}(\tau<\infty,\ A_{\tau-1}\neq a^{\star})=\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{P}(G_{\ell})\leq\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal{T}}\alpha_{\ell}\leq\varepsilon.\qed

Appendix F
Additional Theoretical Results

This appendix collects additional results used to interpret and extend
the main theory: a sharp oracle pairwise benchmark
(
F.1
), an adaptive geometric grid
(
F.2
), a growth-rate optimality result for the
oracle pairwise bet and its finite mixture
(
F.3
), and a top-
k
extension
(
F.4
).

F.1.
Sharp oracle pairwise benchmark

The main text compares the stopping-time upper bound with the minimax
lower bound at the level of rates. Here we isolate a sharper
constant-level statement for an oracle pairwise comparison between the
target and its true runner-up. This result is not a sharp-constant
theorem for the full CITE stopping time; rather, it shows that the
pairwise e-process has the correct leading constant when both the true
runner-up and the oracle betting parameter are available.

Proposition F.1

(Identity and small-gap expansion of
\rho^{\star}
)
.

The oracle pairwise growth rate

\rho^{\star}:=\max_{\lambda\in(0,1)}\bigl[p_{r}\log(1+\lambda)+p_{(2)}\log(1-\lambda)\bigr]

(Proposition
C.6
) coincides exactly with the
forward I-projection KL of
P
onto
H_{0,r}
:

\rho^{\star}=D(P\|P^{\star})=p_{r}\log\frac{2p_{r}}{p_{r}+p_{(2)}}+p_{(2)}\log\frac{2p_{(2)}}{p_{r}+p_{(2)}}.

(27)

In particular, by Lemma
C.9
,

\rho^{\star}=\frac{\delta^{2}}{2(p_{r}+p_{(2)})}+O\left(\frac{\delta^{4}}{(p_{r}+p_{(2)})^{3}}\right)=\frac{\delta^{2}}{4p_{r}}\bigl(1+o(1)\bigr).

Proof.

Substituting
\lambda^{\star}=(p_{r}-p_{(2)})/(p_{r}+p_{(2)})
from
Proposition
C.6
into the definition of

\rho^{\star}
yields the closed form of
D(P\|P^{\star})
in
Lemma
C.9
, since
1\pm\lambda^{\star}=2p_{r}/(p_{r}+p_{(2)})

and
2p_{(2)}/(p_{r}+p_{(2)})
respectively. Both displays now follow
directly from Lemma
C.9
after substituting
q^{\star}=(p_{r}+p_{(2)})/2
.
∎

Theorem F.2

(Sharp constant for the oracle pairwise benchmark)
.

Assume
p_{(2)}>0
,
p_{r}\in(0,1/2]
, and
\delta/p_{r}\to 0
. Suppose

\lambda^{\star}:=\frac{\delta}{p_{r}+p_{(2)}}\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{pw}},\qquad w_{\lambda^{\star}}>0.

Let
a^{\star}\in\arg\max_{a\neq r}p_{a}
, and let

P^{\star}\in H_{0,r}
be the forward I-projection of
P
from
Lemma
C.9
. Define

\rho^{\star}:=\max_{\lambda\in(0,1)}\{p_{r}\log(1+\lambda)+p_{(2)}\log(1-\lambda)\}.

Then

\rho^{\star}=D(P\|P^{\star})=\frac{\delta^{2}}{4p_{r}}\{1+o(1)\}.

For the oracle pairwise hitting time

\sigma_{\mathrm{pw}}^{\star}:=\inf\left\{t\geq 1:w_{\lambda^{\star}}E_{t}^{(r,a^{\star})}(\lambda^{\star})\geq\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}^{-1}\right\},

we have

\mathbb{E}_{P}[\sigma_{\mathrm{pw}}^{\star}]\leq\frac{\log\{1/(\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}w_{\lambda^{\star}})\}}{\rho^{\star}}\{1+o(1)\}.

Conversely, any level-
\varepsilon
sequential certifier
\tau^{\prime}
for

H_{0,r}
with
\mathbb{P}_{P}(\tau^{\prime}<\infty)=1
and

\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau^{\prime}]<\infty
obeys

\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau^{\prime}]\geq\frac{\log(1/\varepsilon)}{\rho^{\star}}.

In particular, if
\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}=c\varepsilon
for a constant

c\in(0,1]
and

\log(1/w_{\lambda^{\star}})=o\{\log(1/\varepsilon)\}
, the oracle
pairwise upper bound and the information lower bound share the leading
constant
4p_{r}/\delta^{2}
.

Proof.

Proposition
F.1
gives

\rho^{\star}=D(P\|P^{\star})=\frac{\delta^{2}}{4p_{r}}\{1+o(1)\}.

(a) Upper bound.

Define

Y_{i}:=\log\{1+\lambda^{\star}Z_{i}^{(r,a^{\star})}\}.

Then the
Y_{i}
are i.i.d., bounded, and

\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y_{i}]=\rho^{\star}>0
. Let

B:=\log\{1/(\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}w_{\lambda^{\star}})\}.

At the hitting time
\sigma_{\mathrm{pw}}^{\star}
,

\sum_{i=1}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{pw}}^{\star}}Y_{i}\geq B.

Moreover, the crossing overshoot is at most the largest positive
increment, so

\sum_{i=1}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{pw}}^{\star}}Y_{i}\leq B+\log(1+\lambda^{\star}).

Since the increments are bounded and the drift is positive, Wald’s
identity gives

\rho^{\star}\mathbb{E}_{P}[\sigma_{\mathrm{pw}}^{\star}]=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{\sigma_{\mathrm{pw}}^{\star}}Y_{i}\right]\leq B+\log(1+\lambda^{\star}).

As
\lambda^{\star}\to 0
in the small-gap regime,

\mathbb{E}_{P}[\sigma_{\mathrm{pw}}^{\star}]\leq\frac{\log\{1/(\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}w_{\lambda^{\star}})\}+o(1)}{\rho^{\star}}.

Substituting the expansion of
\rho^{\star}
proves the oracle upper
bound.

(b) Lower bound.

Apply the sequential change-of-measure argument to the I-projection

P^{\star}\in H_{0,r}
. Type-I validity gives

P^{\star}(\tau^{\prime}<\infty)\leq\varepsilon
, while

\mathbb{P}_{P}(\tau^{\prime}<\infty)=1
. By data processing and
Lemma
C.10
,

\log(1/\varepsilon)\leq\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau^{\prime}]D(P\|P^{\star})=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau^{\prime}]\rho^{\star}.

Hence

\mathbb{E}_{P}[\tau^{\prime}]\geq\frac{\log(1/\varepsilon)}{\rho^{\star}}.

(c) Ratio.

With
\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}=c\varepsilon
,

\frac{\log(1/(\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}w_{\lambda^{\star}}))}{\log(1/\varepsilon)}=1+\frac{\log(1/(cw_{\lambda^{\star}}))}{\log(1/\varepsilon)}\to 1

using
\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}=c\varepsilon
and

\log(1/w_{\lambda^{\star}})=o\{\log(1/\varepsilon)\}
.
∎

Remark F.3

(Scope of the oracle result)
.

Theorem
F.2
is an oracle benchmark for comparing
r

with its true runner-up. It is not a sharp-constant theorem for the
full CITE stopping time, which also uses the empirical runner-up, the
LCB–unseen check, and a finite grid.

F.2.
Adaptive geometric grid

Condition
4.2
requires a grid point in

[\delta/8,\delta/4]
, which depends on the unknown gap
\delta
. A
geometric grid gives a uniform version of the same rate over all

\delta\geq\delta_{0}
.

Condition F.4

(Geometric grid)
.

For a fixed minimum gap
\delta_{0}\in(0,1]
, set

\Lambda_{\mathrm{pw}}^{\mathrm{geo}}:=\{2^{-k}:1\leq k\leq K\},\qquad K:=\left\lceil\log_{2}(8/\delta_{0})\right\rceil,

with uniform weights
w_{\lambda}=1/K
.

Theorem F.5

(Adaptive grid rate)
.

Suppose Assumption
2.1
holds and CITE uses

\Lambda_{\mathrm{pw}}^{\mathrm{geo}}
in the pairwise component. Keep
the LCB-grid and selected-weight assumptions of
Theorem
4.3
. Then, uniformly over all

P
with modal gap
\delta\geq\delta_{0}
,

\mathbb{E}[\tau]=O\left(\frac{\log(1/\varepsilon)+\log(\log(8/\delta_{0})\vee e)+\log(1/\delta)}{\delta^{2}}+\frac{\log(1/\varepsilon)+\log(1/p_{r})}{p_{r}}\right),

with constants independent of
\delta,p_{r}
, and
|\mathcal{A}|
.

Proof.

Fix
P
with
\delta\geq\delta_{0}
and set

k^{\star}:=\left\lfloor\log_{2}(8/\delta)\right\rfloor,\qquad\lambda:=2^{-k^{\star}}.

Then
\lambda\in[\delta/8,\delta/4]
and, since

\delta\geq\delta_{0}
, we have
k^{\star}\leq K
. Hence

\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{pw}}^{\mathrm{geo}}
.

The proof of Theorem
4.3
applies with this grid
point. The only change is the pairwise mixture weight:

\log\frac{1}{\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}w_{\lambda}}=\log\frac{1}{\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}}+\log K=\log\frac{1}{\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}}+O\{\log(\log(8/\delta_{0})\vee e)\}.

Substituting this into the pairwise part of the stopping-time bound,
and keeping the LCB–unseen part unchanged, proves the display.
∎

Remark F.6
.

The geometric grid removes the need to know
\delta
exactly, but it
still requires a lower design scale
\delta_{0}
. Removing this design
parameter would require a separate adaptive-betting analysis, which we
do not pursue here.

F.3.
Growth-rate optimality of the oracle pairwise bet

We record a class-restricted growth-rate optimality property for the
oracle pairwise bet, in the GRO/GROW sense of

Grünwald et al. 2024
. The comparison is restricted to predictable
linear bets in the pairwise statistic.

Definition F.7

(Class-restricted GRO/GROW)
.

Fix a pair
(r,a)
with
r\neq a
, a class
\mathcal{Q}
of alternatives, and a
class
\mathcal{E}
of e-processes for the pairwise null

H_{0}^{(a)}=\{P:p_{r}\leq p_{a}\}
. An element
E^{\star}\in\mathcal{E}
is

GRO/GROW within
\mathcal{E}
with respect to
\mathcal{Q}
if, for every fixed
horizon
T
,

E^{\star}\in\arg\max_{E\in\mathcal{E}}\inf_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathbb{E}_{Q}[\log E_{T}].

Theorem F.8

(GRO bet and mixture near-optimality)
.

Let
\mathcal{E}_{Z}
denote the class of processes

E_{t}=\prod_{i=1}^{t}(1+\lambda_{i}Z_{i}^{(r,a)})
with predictable
bets
\lambda_{i}\in[0,1)
measurable with respect to

\mathcal{F}_{i-1}:=\sigma(X_{1},\ldots,X_{i-1})
.
Every
E\in\mathcal{E}_{Z}
is an e-process for the composite null

H_{0}^{(a)}=\{P:p_{r}\leq p_{a}\}
in the sense of
Definition
F.7
.

Fix a simple alternative
Q
with
\mathbb{P}_{Q}(X=r)=p
,

\mathbb{P}_{Q}(X=a)=q
, and
p>q>0
(the boundary case
q=0
is
degenerate:
\lambda^{\star}=1
lies on the boundary of the open
betting interval
[0,1)
, so the supremum is approached only in the
limit
\lambda\uparrow 1
and is not attained in
\mathcal{E}_{Z}
),
and define

\lambda^{\star}:=\frac{p-q}{p+q},\qquad\rho^{\star}:=p\log(1+\lambda^{\star})+q\log(1-\lambda^{\star}).

(a)

GRO bet.

For every
T\geq 1
and every
E\in\mathcal{E}_{Z}
,

\mathbb{E}_{Q}[\log E_{T}]\leq T\rho^{\star}
, with equality if and only if

\lambda_{i}=\lambda^{\star}
holds
Q
-almost surely for every

i\leq T
. In particular,
E_{t}^{(r,a)}(\lambda^{\star})
is the unique
growth-rate maximizer within
\mathcal{E}_{Z}
under the simple alternative
Q
.

(b)

Mixture near-optimality.

For any finite grid
\Lambda_{\mathrm{pw}}\subset(0,1)
with

\lambda^{\star}\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{pw}}
and positive weights

(w_{\lambda})
summing to at most
1
, the mixture

E_{t}^{(r,a)}:=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{pw}}}w_{\lambda}E_{t}^{(r,a)}(\lambda)

satisfies, for every
T\geq 1
,

T\rho^{\star}-\log\frac{1}{w_{\lambda^{\star}}}\leq\mathbb{E}_{Q}[\log E_{T}^{(r,a)}]\leq T\rho^{\star}.

The mixture therefore has the same asymptotic expected log-growth rate
as the oracle fixed-
\lambda^{\star}
bet, with the additive log-weight
term
\log(1/w_{\lambda^{\star}})
.

Proof.

Preliminary: e-process property.

For any
P\in H_{0}^{(a)}
,
X_{i}
is independent of
\mathcal{F}_{i-1}
with

\mathbb{E}_{P}[Z_{i}^{(r,a)}]=p_{r}-p_{a}\leq 0
, so

\mathbb{E}_{P}[1+\lambda_{i}Z_{i}^{(r,a)}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}]=1+\lambda_{i}(p_{r}-p_{a})\leq 1

(
\lambda_{i}\geq 0
). Thus
(E_{t})
is a nonneg. supermartingale with

E_{0}=1
, and optional stopping gives
\mathbb{E}_{P}[E_{\tau}]\leq 1
for any
stopping time
\tau
.

Part (a): GRO bet.

Fix
E\in\mathcal{E}_{Z}
. For each
i
,
\lambda_{i}
is

\mathcal{F}_{i-1}
-measurable and
X_{i}
is independent of
\mathcal{F}_{i-1}
under

Q
, taking values
r
,
a
, and “other” with probabilities
p
,

q
, and
1-p-q
(in which last case
Z_{i}=0
and the log term
vanishes). Conditioning on
\mathcal{F}_{i-1}
,

\mathbb{E}_{Q}\bigl[\log(1+\lambda_{i}Z_{i}^{(r,a)})\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\bigr]=p\log(1+\lambda_{i})+q\log(1-\lambda_{i})=:\rho(\lambda_{i}).

Summing over
i=1,\ldots,T
via the tower property gives

\mathbb{E}_{Q}[\log E_{T}]=\sum_{i=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{Q}[\rho(\lambda_{i})].

(28)

By Proposition
C.6
,
\rho
is strictly concave
on
(0,1)
with unique maximiser
\lambda^{\star}
, so

\rho(\lambda_{i})\leq\rho^{\star}
pointwise. Substituting
into (
28
) yields
\mathbb{E}_{Q}[\log E_{T}]\leq T\rho^{\star}
,
with equality iff
\lambda_{i}=\lambda^{\star}

Q
-a.s. for each
i
.

Lower bound for part (b).

For every
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{pw}}
,
E_{t}^{(r,a)}(\lambda)>0
,
hence
E_{T}^{(r,a)}\geq w_{\lambda^{\star}}E_{T}^{(r,a)}(\lambda^{\star})

Q
-a.s. Taking logs and
Q
-expectations and applying (a) to the
constant bet
\lambda_{i}\equiv\lambda^{\star}
:

\mathbb{E}_{Q}[\log E_{T}^{(r,a)}]\geq\log w_{\lambda^{\star}}+T\rho^{\star}=T\rho^{\star}-\log(1/w_{\lambda^{\star}}).

Upper bound for part (b), via variational duality.

Let
P^{\star}
be the boundary distribution

P^{\star}(X=r)=P^{\star}(X=a):=\tfrac{p+q}{2},\qquad P^{\star}(X=b):=Q(X=b)\text{ for }b\notin\{r,a\}.

The Lagrangian argument of Lemma
C.9
, applied to

H_{0}^{(a)}=\{P:p_{r}\leq p_{a}\}
in place of
H_{0,r}
, identifies

P^{\star}
as the forward I-projection of
Q
onto
H_{0}^{(a)}
, with
the closed form

D(Q\|P^{\star})=p\log\frac{2p}{p+q}+q\log\frac{2q}{p+q}=p\log(1+\lambda^{\star})+q\log(1-\lambda^{\star})=\rho^{\star},

(29)

the second equality from

1\pm\lambda^{\star}=2p/(p+q)
and
2q/(p+q)
.

Writing
W:=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{pw}}}w_{\lambda}\leq 1
,

E_{T}^{(r,a)}
is an e-process for
H_{0}^{(a)}
(mixture of
e-processes), so
\mathbb{E}_{P^{\star}}[E_{T}^{(r,a)}]\leq W\leq 1
.
Decomposing the
Q
-expectation via change of measure and applying
Jensen to the concave
\log
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{Q}[\log E_{T}^{(r,a)}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{Q}\left[\log\left(E_{T}^{(r,a)}\cdot\frac{d(P^{\star})^{T}}{dQ^{T}}\right)\right]+\mathbb{E}_{Q}\left[\log\frac{dQ^{T}}{d(P^{\star})^{T}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\log\mathbb{E}_{P^{\star}}[E_{T}^{(r,a)}]+D(Q^{T}\|(P^{\star})^{T})\leq 0+TD(Q\|P^{\star})=T\rho^{\star},

where the last line uses
\mathbb{E}_{P^{\star}}[E_{T}^{(r,a)}]\leq 1
,
tensorisation of KL, and (
29
). Combining with the lower
bound finishes the proof.
∎

F.4.
Top-
k
mode certification

Fix a target set
S\subseteq\mathcal{A}
of size
k\geq 1
with

\mathcal{A}\setminus S\neq\emptyset
(so that the outsider competitor
set is meaningful).
We extend CITE to certify that
S
contains the top-
k
categories
in the sense

H_{1,S}^{(k)}:=\bigl\{P:\min_{s\in S}p_{s}>\sup_{a\notin S}p_{a}\bigr\},\qquad H_{0,S}^{(k)}:=\neg H_{1,S}^{(k)}.

Top-
k
CITE.

Let

\delta_{S}:=\min_{s\in S}p_{s}-\sup_{a\notin S}p_{a},\qquad p_{S}:=\min_{s\in S}p_{s}.

For each
s\in S
and
a\notin S
, define

E_{t}^{(s,a)}
as in Definition
3.1
, and let

L_{t}(s)
be the LCB for
p_{s}
.

At time
t
, if
\mathcal{A}_{t}\setminus S\neq\emptyset
, define

\widehat{a}_{t}^{\mathrm{out}}\in\arg\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}_{t}\setminus S}N_{t}(a)

with deterministic tie-breaking. Define

\mathsf{PW}_{t}^{(k)}:=\begin{cases}\mathrm{true},&\mathcal{A}_{t}\setminus S=\emptyset,\\[2.84526pt]
\left\{\displaystyle\min_{s\in S}\widetilde{E}_{t}^{(s,\widehat{a}_{t}^{\mathrm{out}})}\geq\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}^{-1}\right\},&\mathcal{A}_{t}\setminus S\neq\emptyset.\end{cases}

The top-
k
CITE stopping time is

\tau^{(k)}:=\inf\left\{t\geq 1:\mathsf{PW}_{t}^{(k)}\text{ and }\min_{s\in S}L_{t}(s)>U_{t}\right\}.

(30)

Theorem F.9

(Top-
k
Type-I and power)
.

Run the top-
k
CITE (
30
) under the same
component-budget and selected-grid-weight bounded-below assumptions
as Theorem
4.3
, with the same per-component
budget as the single-target CITE,

\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}+\alpha_{r}+\alpha_{u}\leq\varepsilon
.

(a)

Type-I:
Under Assumption
2.1
,

\sup_{P\in H_{0,S}^{(k)}}\mathbb{P}_{P}(\tau^{(k)}<\infty)\leq\varepsilon
.

(b)

Power:
Suppose
\delta_{S}>0
and the grids satisfy the
analogue of Condition
4.2
with
\delta_{S}
in place of

\delta
. Define the threshold

T_{\mathrm{cl}}^{(k)}:=C_{0}\left(\frac{\log(k/\varepsilon)+\log(1/\delta_{S})}{\delta_{S}^{2}}+\frac{\log(k/\varepsilon)+\log(1/p_{S})}{p_{S}}\right)

for a universal constant
C_{0}
. For
fixed

k
, as

\varepsilon\to 0
,

\mathbb{E}[\tau^{(k)}]\leq T_{\mathrm{cl}}^{(k)}\bigl(1+o(1)\bigr)=O(T_{\mathrm{cl}}^{(k)}).

The upper bound extends to growing
k
provided

k=O(T_{\mathrm{cl}}^{(k)})
, e.g. when
\delta_{S}^{-2}\log(k/\varepsilon)

dominates
k
.

Proof.

(a) Type-I.

Fix any
P\in H_{0,S}^{(k)}
. Choose

s^{\star}\in S
such that

p_{s^{\star}}=\min_{s\in S}p_{s}.

Since
P\in H_{0,S}^{(k)}
,

p_{s^{\star}}\leq\sup_{a\notin S}p_{a}.

If this supremum is positive, Lemma
B.1
gives

a^{\star}\notin S
with
p_{a^{\star}}\geq p_{s^{\star}}
. If
p_{s^{\star}}=0
, any outsider
a^{\star}\notin S
can be used.

Decompose

\{\tau^{(k)}<\infty\}\subseteq\underbrace{\{\tau^{(k)}<\infty,a^{\star}\in\mathcal{A}_{\tau^{(k)}}\}}_{E_{1}}\cup\underbrace{\{\tau^{(k)}<\infty,a^{\star}\notin\mathcal{A}_{\tau^{(k)}}\}}_{E_{2}}.

On
E_{1}
, the procedure’s pairwise condition holds at
\tau^{(k)}
for

every

s\in S
, including the witness
s^{\star}
:

E_{\tau^{(k)}}^{(s^{\star},\widehat{a}^{\mathrm{out}}_{\tau^{(k)}})}\geq 1/\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}
.
Runner-up monotonicity (Lemma
B.3
) restricted to
outsiders (since
a^{\star}\in\mathcal{A}_{\tau^{(k)}}\setminus S
implies

N_{\tau^{(k)}}(a^{\star})\leq N_{\tau^{(k)}}(\widehat{a}^{\mathrm{out}}_{\tau^{(k)}})
) gives

E_{\tau^{(k)}}^{(s^{\star},a^{\star})}\geq E_{\tau^{(k)}}^{(s^{\star},\widehat{a}^{\mathrm{out}}_{\tau^{(k)}})}\geq 1/\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}
.
Since
s^{\star},a^{\star}
are deterministic and

(E_{t}^{(s^{\star},a^{\star})})_{t\geq 0}
is an NSM with
initial value
\leq 1
under
p_{s^{\star}}\leq p_{a^{\star}}

(Corollary
3.2
), Ville’s inequality (
11
)
yields
\mathbb{P}(E_{1})\leq\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}
.

On
E_{2}
, condition (b) at
\tau^{(k)}
holds for the witness

s^{\star}
:
L_{\tau^{(k)}}(s^{\star})>U_{\tau^{(k)}}
. Define the two
failure events

F_{r}:=\{\exists t:L_{t}(s^{\star})>p_{s^{\star}}\},\qquad F_{u}:=\{\exists a,t:N_{t}(a)=0,\ p_{a}>U_{t}\}.

Proposition
3.4
(applied to the single index

s^{\star}
) gives
\mathbb{P}(F_{r})\leq\alpha_{r}
, and
Proposition
3.6
gives
\mathbb{P}(F_{u})\leq\alpha_{u}
.
On
E_{2}\setminus(F_{r}\cup F_{u})
,

L_{\tau^{(k)}}(s^{\star})\leq p_{s^{\star}}
and

p_{a^{\star}}\leq U_{\tau^{(k)}}
(since
a^{\star}\notin\mathcal{A}_{\tau^{(k)}}
),
yielding the contradiction

p_{a^{\star}}\leq U_{\tau^{(k)}}<L_{\tau^{(k)}}(s^{\star})\leq p_{s^{\star}}
,
contrary to
p_{a^{\star}}\geq p_{s^{\star}}
. Hence

\mathbb{P}(E_{2})\leq\alpha_{r}+\alpha_{u}
.

A union bound gives

\mathbb{P}(\tau^{(k)}<\infty)\leq\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}+\alpha_{r}+\alpha_{u}\leq\varepsilon
.

(b) Power.

The single-target CITE of Definition
3.7
applied to a
target
s\in S
is
not
appropriate here, because its pairwise
check ranges over all of
\mathcal{A}\setminus\{s\}
and may include
competitors
s^{\prime}\in S
with
p_{s^{\prime}}\geq p_{s}
, on which the pairwise
null is true; the resulting stopping time would be
+\infty
a.s.
Instead, fix
s\in S
and consider the

outsider-restricted single-target CITE for target
s
, defined
exactly as Definition
3.7
except that the empirical
runner-up
\widehat{a}_{t}
is replaced by

\widehat{a}_{t}^{\mathrm{out}}:=\arg\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}_{t}\setminus S}N_{t}(a)

and the pairwise threshold is
k/\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}
, the LCB
threshold is
k/\alpha_{r}
, the unseen budget
\alpha_{u}
is unchanged.
We adopt the same convention as for the actual top-
k
procedure: if

\mathcal{A}_{t}\setminus S=\emptyset
, the per-
s
pairwise condition is
treated as vacuously satisfied at round
t
.
The tightened thresholds
k/\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}
and
k/\alpha_{r}
are

strictly stronger sufficient
requirements than the actual top-
k

stopping criteria (which use the original thresholds
1/\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}

and the LCB inequality
L_{t}(s)>U_{t}
); they are introduced solely so
that a Bonferroni union bound over
s\in S
on the per-target failure
events yields a uniform
\varepsilon
-level conclusion.
Write
\tau_{s}^{\mathrm{out}}
for its stopping time.

We use a fixed-time tail bound rather than a max-of-first-hitting-times
argument (the latter fails because pairwise e-processes and LCBs are
not monotone in
t
). For each fixed
t\geq 1
,

\{\tau^{(k)}>t\}\subseteq\bigcup_{s\in S}\left(\left\{\mathcal{A}_{t}\setminus S\neq\emptyset,\ E_{t}^{(s,\widehat{a}_{t}^{\mathrm{out}})}<\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}^{-1}\right\}\cup\{L_{t}(s)\leq U_{t}\}\right).

(31)

Apply the proof of Theorem
4.3
to the

outsider-restricted single-target CITE for target
s
, with

\mathcal{A}\setminus\{r\}
replaced by
\mathcal{A}\setminus S
in the
pairwise/LCB analysis (the unseen bound
U_{t}
is unchanged since it is
already over all of
\mathcal{A}
): the runner-up monotonicity
(Lemma
B.3
) holds within any subset, the LCB
construction is local to target
s
, and the effective modal gap

p_{s}-\max_{a\notin S}p_{a}\geq\delta_{S}
and target mass
p_{s}\geq p_{S}
.
With per-
s
thresholds tightened to
k/\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}
for the
pairwise check and
k/\alpha_{r}
for the LCB (a Bonferroni split over

|S|=k
), the same McDiarmid + Bernstein arguments give, for each
fixed
s
and each
t\geq T_{\mathrm{cl}}^{(k)}
,

\mathbb{P}\bigl(E_{t}^{(s,\widehat{a}_{t}^{\mathrm{out}})}<1/\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}\text{or}L_{t}(s)\leq U_{t}\bigr)\leq 2(et)^{-2},

where

T_{\mathrm{cl}}^{(k)}=C_{0}\bigl((\log(k/\varepsilon)+\log(1/\delta_{S}))/\delta_{S}^{2}+(\log(k/\varepsilon)+\log(1/p_{S}))/p_{S}\bigr)
for a universal constant
C_{0}
.
Substituting into (
31
) and applying a union
bound over
s\in S
,

\mathbb{P}(\tau^{(k)}>t)\leq 2k(et)^{-2}\qquad\text{for all }t\geq T_{\mathrm{cl}}^{(k)}.

The tail-sum identity (
\sum_{t\geq T}1/t^{2}\leq 2/T
for
T\geq 2
)
yields

\mathbb{E}[\tau^{(k)}]\leq T_{\mathrm{cl}}^{(k)}+\sum_{t>T_{\mathrm{cl}}^{(k)}}2k(et)^{-2}\leq T_{\mathrm{cl}}^{(k)}\bigl(1+o(1)\bigr)\quad(\varepsilon\to 0,\ k\text{ fixed}),

which gives the claimed bound.
∎

Remark F.10
.

The top-
k
extension preserves the runner-up reduction within the
“outsider” set, so the per-round cost is
k
pairwise checks and

k
LCB updates, plus one shared unseen bound.
The case
k=1
recovers CITE of
Definition
3.7
, with Type-I and rate guarantees given
by Theorems
4.1
and
4.3

respectively.

G.2.
Results for All Reported Settings

Figure 3.
Certification rate versus sample budget
N
for CITE, W-CITE,
fixed-sample Bonferroni, the sample-split fixed-target test KR

(
Kim and Ramdas 2025
)
, and the leader-tracking MMC

(
Cordero-Encinar and Duncan 2025
)
, across all five reported simulation
settings. The panels for Settings 3 and 4 use zoomed vertical axes
because all methods remain low-power over the evaluated budget range.
CITE matches or exceeds Bonferroni in most regimes, with the easy
concentrated Setting 2 saturating quickly for both methods. Settings
3–4 are also the regimes where KR and MMC show nonzero false
certifications under Case B (Table
3
).

Type-I error control.

Across all five settings and all budgets

N\in\{64,128,256,512,1024,2048\}
, CITE, W-CITE, and Bonferroni have
empirical false-certification rate exactly zero in every Case B
experiment. KR also has zero false certifications in Settings 1, 2,
and 5, with only small nonzero rates on Settings 3–4. MMC shows
nonzero false certifications in the same two regimes, illustrating that
leader-tracking certificates are not designed for the fixed-target null
considered here. Table
3
reports the full
per-budget Case B false-certification rates.

Power (Case A).

Tables
4
–
8
report certification rates
for all five settings. Setting 2 saturates quickly for all methods,
whereas Settings 3 and 4 are the most difficult regimes. Setting 5
highlights a diffuse-tail regime in which CITE substantially outperforms
MMC at moderate budgets. Bonferroni can be slightly more powerful in
the easiest concentrated regime at the smallest budget, but CITE
matches or improves on it in the remaining reported regimes and
budgets.

G.3.
Bottleneck Analysis

Figure 4.
Component-level diagnostics for the two terms in the
stopping-time upper bound of Theorem
4.3
, on
log-log axes. Dashed reference
lines show the theoretical slopes. (a) Fixing
p_{r}=0.24
and
varying
\delta
, the pairwise component

\mathbb{E}[\tau_{\mathrm{pw}}]
(first time the pairwise e-value crosses

1/\alpha_{\mathrm{pw}}
) is consistent with the
\delta^{-2}
term.
(b) Fixing
\delta=0.05
and varying
p_{r}
, the LCB/unseen
component
\mathbb{E}[\tau_{\mathrm{lu}}]
(first time

L_{t}(r)>U_{t}
) is consistent with the
p_{r}^{-1}
term.

G.4.
Logistic Weight Model Saturation

As discussed in Section
6
, the logistic weight
model can saturate when
K
is large. For Settings 1 and 5, sweeping

\beta\in\{0.5,1,2,4,8,16\}
produces certification rates and stopping
times that are nearly unchanged relative to unweighted CITE. The
effective gap ratio
\Delta_{w}/\mu_{r}
is essentially the same as

\delta/p_{r}
for
\beta\geq 2
. This occurs because the top categories
are already assigned weights close to one, so the logistic
transformation does not substantially separate the mode from its
runner-up.

G.5.
Rank-Based Weight Model

The rank-based model assigns
E[W\mid X=a]=0.95\cdot e^{-\gamma\cdot\mathrm{rank}(a)}

for the top
K_{0}=10
categories and
w_{\mathrm{low}}=0.1
otherwise,
with additive
\mathrm{Uniform}(-0.05,0.05)
noise clipped to
[0.01,1]
.

Table
9
reports detailed results for Setting 5 at

N=512
. We omit a separate figure here to avoid duplicating
Figure
3
; the table directly shows how the effective
weighted gap and stopping time change with the rank-decay parameter.

In this synthetic rank-based model, performance improves as the
effective gap ratio
\Delta_{w}/\mu_{r}
increases. A modest

\gamma=0.2
already reaches full certification in this sweep and
reduces
\bar{\tau}
by about 40%; at
\gamma=1.0
, the reduction is
about 80%. For Setting 1, the rank-based model with

\gamma\geq 0.1
reaches 99.6% certification at
N=128
(versus 88.6%
unweighted) with
\bar{\tau}\approx 72
(versus 92).

Appendix H
LLM Self-Consistency Details

Table
10
reports the certification rate averaged
across problems for each (model, dataset, budget) under Case A
(target = mode); Table
11
reports the
corresponding Case B (target = runner-up) certification rates as a
sanity check on Type-I error. For each
(model, problem) pair we draw 1,000 i.i.d. samples, then bootstrap
500 replicates of each budget
N\in\{64,128,256,512,1024\}
from
the empirical answer pool to compute certification rates.

H.1.
Model settings

Sampling parameters.

Qwen3-30B-A3B-Instruct-2507:
temperature=0.7
,
top_p=0.8
,
top_k=20
. gpt-oss-20b:
temperature=0.4
,
top_p=0.9
,
top_k=20
,
reasoning_effort=low
.

Response probability weight for W-CITE.

For each sample
i
, let
(y_{1},\ldots,y_{T_{i}})
denote the tokens of the extracted answer span (the final
\boxed{...}
contents for AIME and FrontierScience, the
Triage:
\langle
acuity
\rangle\mid
Disposition:
\langle
disposition
\rangle
string for ER-REASON). The W-CITE weight is the response probability used by the weighted variant of self-consistency
(
Wang et al. 2023
)
,

w_{i}=\prod_{t=1}^{T_{i}}p_{\theta}\left(y_{t}\mid y_{<t},\text{prompt}\right),

i.e., the model’s own likelihood of the extracted answer under token-by-token factorization. Weights are not renormalized across samples.

H.2.
Dataset protocols

AIME 2026.

All 30 problems of AIME 2026
(
Dekoninck et al. 2026
)
(AIME I and AIME II combined), each with an integer gold answer in
[0,999]
. Prompt is the problem statement with a per-model suffix: “Solve in under 100 tokens. Put your final answer within
\boxed{}
.” (Qwen3-30B-A3B-Instruct-2507) or “Do not show your reasoning. Put only the final answer within
\boxed{}
.” (gpt-oss-20b). Answers are extracted from the last
\boxed{...}
and matched by exact integer equality.

FrontierScience-Olympiad.

FrontierScience-Olympiad
(
Wang et al. 2026
)
contains 100 olympiad-level physics, chemistry, and biology problems with numeric or symbolic answers. We use a 10-problem subset obtained by taking every 10th problem (problems 10, 20, …, 100). Prompt suffix is the same as for AIME 2026. Each extracted answer is compared against the gold by an LLM self-judge (the generating model decides whether its own output is equivalent to the gold).

ER-REASON.

ER-REASON
(
Mehandru et al. 2025
)
contains 3,984 emergency-room encounters. We use the 72-encounter subset with expert-authored clinical rationales, which the paper designates as the primary evaluation set. Both models use a two-turn chat prompt in place of the
\boxed{}
suffix. Turn 1 asks the model to assign an ESI acuity level (1–5) from the chief complaint, demographics, and triage vital signs. Turn 2, which echoes the Turn 1 acuity, asks for the disposition (one of 12 labels) given the full ED provider note and all historical notes. The final answer is parsed as the fixed string “Triage:
\langle
acuity
\rangle\mid
Disposition:
\langle
disposition
\rangle
”.

.

Appendix I
Reproducibility Statement

Hardware and inference server.

Real-LLM experiments are conducted using NVIDIA A100 80 GB GPUs with vLLM as the inference server.

Data and code access.

All code will be open-sourced upon publication. Datasets and models are publicly available (Table
18
). ER-REASON requires PhysioNet credentialed access.

Name

Reference

Models

Qwen3-30B-A3B-Instruct-2507
(
Qwen Team 2025
)

https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3-30B-A3B-Instruct-2507

gpt-oss-20b
(
OpenAI 2025
)

https://huggingface.co/openai/gpt-oss-20b

Datasets

AIME 2026
(
Dekoninck et al. 2026
)

https://huggingface.co/datasets/MathArena/aime_2026

FrontierScience-Olympiad
(
Wang et al. 2026
)

https://huggingface.co/datasets/openai/frontierscience

ER-REASON
(
Mehandru et al. 2025
)

https://physionet.org/content/er-reason/1.0.0/

Table 18.
List of models and datasets used in the LLM self-consistency experiments.
